Discussion Name:
Should jury trials be abolished?

1. Thesis:
Jury trials should be abolished.

  1.1. Con:
  Juries provide a valuable check and balance on the power of the government.

    1.1.1. Con:
    US juries are intended to curb the power of the federal government (p. 123 [1]). Since the majority of US criminal cases are held in state rather than federal courts [2], most juries do not act as a check against the government.

      1.1.1.1. Pro:
      In most US states, defendants can waive the right [3] to a jury trial in favor of a bench trial if they choose, suggesting that juries are not an essential check and balance against state power.

        1.1.1.1.1. Con:
        The ability to waive one's right to a jury trial does not detract from the ability of juries to check state power, as a defendant has the opportunity [4] to be tried by a jury if they so choose.

        1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
        The percentage of cases that are resolved by jury trial is very low [5] in most states and has continued to decrease.

      1.1.1.2. Con:
      Federal crimes are generally more serious and carry longer sentences [6] than state crimes, so juries remain an essential check on federal power regardless of whether states also employ them.

        1.1.1.2.1. Con:
        Juries manifestly do not act as an effective check on federal power given the 99.6% federal conviction rate [7].

        1.1.1.2.2. Con:
        Federal juries do not effectively check federal power, as they acquit significantly less often [8] than federal judges.

        1.1.1.2.3. Pro:
        Defendants in federal cases do not have the right to waive a jury trial, and can only do so with government permission and for good reason (p. 1093 [9]). This supports the interpretation of juries as an essential check on the substantial prosecutory power of the federal government.

    1.1.2. Pro:
    Public participation in the justice system in the form of juries is one of the few public checks on government power and ensures judicial accountability (p. 2179 [10]).

      1.1.2.1. Con:
      In the absence of juries, public participation in the justice system in the form of audiences [11] and media coverage [12] would continue to check government power and promote judicial accountability.

        1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
        The resources currently used to support juries could instead be appropriated for improving and expanding media access to trials, allowing for wider public awareness and scrutiny (p. 15 [13]).

        1.1.2.1.2. Con:
        Audiences and media coverage do not have as direct or profound of an impact compared with the deliberations and verdicts of juries (p. 12 [14]).

      1.1.2.2. Con:
      Powerful non-government actors, such as the ACLU [15], already exert substantial political power and oversight within the US legal system and help to ensure accountability, regardless of the existence of juries.

        1.1.2.2.1. Pro:
        The ACLU has a long history of victories [16] in influential, high-profile civil rights cases.

        1.1.2.2.2. Con:
        On some of the biggest stages and most important cases, these groups have failed to hold [17] the US justice system accountable, suggesting that they are not a suitable replacement for juries.

          1.1.2.2.2.1. Pro:
          The ACLU is a partisan organization [18], and as such cannot reasonably represent the American people in the same way that citizen juries can.

          1.1.2.2.2.2. Con:
          The case referenced in the parent claim, like all cases heard by the SCOTUS [19], was tried by justices rather than a jury. Hence, juries cannot act as a check on government power in such cases.

      1.1.2.3. Pro:
      Countries that use jury trials are correlated with stronger judicial restraints [20] on other branches of the government.

        1.1.2.3.1. Con:
        The relationship between the two is strictly correlative and could be attributable to an unidentified common factor.

        1.1.2.3.2. Pro:
        Juries composed of system outsiders encourage other branches of the government to act appropriately, since it is more difficult to pull favor with random citizens than with fellow elected or appointed government officials (p. 1 [21]).

      1.1.2.4. Pro:
      Juries are a bulwark against tyranny, similar to plebiscites [22] and referendums [23] (p. 1 [24]).

        1.1.2.4.1. Pro:
        Grand juries [25] help ensure that the government does not frivolously prosecute individuals without substantial evidence.

          1.1.2.4.1.1. Pro:
          In the US, the federal government and half of the states require a grand jury indictment [26] before prosecuting a serious crime.

          1.1.2.4.1.2. Con:
          Prosecutors have an inordinate amount of influence over grand juries and have often been found guilty of misconduct upon review (pp. 134-135 [27]).

        1.1.2.4.2. Con:
        Juries are simply too small to represent popular opinion in the same way as a plebiscite or a referendum [28], which typically involves most of the voting population.

          1.1.2.4.2.1. Con:
          Regardless of the differences in the way they represent popular opinion, all three forms of democratic representation allow ordinary citizens to voice their opinions on specific issues in society.

          1.1.2.4.2.2. Con:
          Since referendums and plebiscites are often poorly written or ask the wrong question [29], they are equally poor methods of deciding public opinion.

          1.1.2.4.2.3. Pro:
          Juries provide a verdict on a single case; referendums and plebiscites are wide-reaching and able to influence national policy [30].

        1.1.2.4.3. Pro:
        Ancient Greek courts were conducted solely by jurors, who were randomly selected from all classes (p. 3 [31]), rather than by judges in order to balance the power of the government (pp. 130-131 [32]). Modern juries fulfill a similar role by empowering regular citizens.

        1.1.2.4.4. Pro:
        The jury system places the burden of proof on the government to prove the defendant's guilt, beyond a reasonable doubt [33], to a group of citizens rather than government officials. This protects defendants from government power.

          1.1.2.4.4.1. Con:
          Bench verdicts are subject to the same limitation. The prosecution must prove the defendant's guilt to either a judge or a jury.

          1.1.2.4.4.2. Pro:
          If government officials alone determine a defendant's guilt, then collusion between government bodies becomes possible, particularly in cases where a government agency is one party in the trial (p. 17 [34]).

          1.1.2.4.4.3. Con:
          Guilt beyond a reasonable doubt is only required in criminal cases. In civil cases [35], the burden of proof is the preponderance of evidence.

        1.1.2.4.5. Con:
        Countries that have abolished [36] jury trials have not descended into tyranny.

          1.1.2.4.5.1. Pro:
          Despite having abolished trial by jury [37] in both civil and criminal cases, the German legal system is consistently ranked among the world's best by the World Justice Project (p. 16 [38]).

    1.1.3. Pro:
    Juries can acquit guilty defendants [39] when they believe a law is wrong or that punishment would be unfair. This acts as a check on legislative power.

      1.1.3.1. Pro:
      Jury nullification [40] is an important check against the government and unjust laws.

        1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
        Jurors cannot be penalized for voting in accordance with their conscience [41]; they can acquit defendants based solely on their disagreement with the law.

        1.1.3.1.2. Con:
        Though the right to jury nullification exists [42], there are barriers in place that prevent it from being exercised effectively.

        1.1.3.1.3. Pro:
        Juries have historically made frequent use of jury nullification to uphold true justice.

          1.1.3.1.3.1. Pro:
          Jury nullification was used to prevent the conviction [43] of fugitive slaves and those aiding their escape.

          1.1.3.1.3.2. Con:
          Jury nullification has also been used to prevent [44] the conviction [45] of lynch mobs.

          1.1.3.1.3.3. Pro:
          Jury nullification was used by juries to prevent the conviction of Vietnam War [46] protesters and draft dodgers.

          1.1.3.1.3.4. Pro:
          Juries have used nullification to protect free speech and freedom of association by refusing to convict [47] union members and labor organizers.

          1.1.3.1.3.5. Con:
          Jury nullification is unjust because it allows jurors to violate their oath [48]: to consider only the evidence presented and the instructions of the judge.

          1.1.3.1.3.6. Con:
          Jury nullification is only used in 3-4% of criminal trials [49].

      1.1.3.2. Con:
      Only judges should be able to override laws passed by democratically elected legislatures.

        1.1.3.2.1. Con:
        The verdict of a jury applies only to the specific case at hand; a jury verdict cannot change a law [50].

          1.1.3.2.1.1. Con:
          Although juries cannot directly affect laws, frequent jury nullification of a crime is often a precursor to legislative change. For example, the high acquittal rate [51] in alcohol-related cases contributed to the repeal of the 18th Amendment [52], which prohibited the sale of alcohol in the US (p. 71 [53]).

          1.1.3.2.1.2. Pro:
          Juries do not determine laws; they only determine if the government has proven that a law was violated [54].

        1.1.3.2.2. Pro:
        In the US, only judges have the power to override democratically enacted legislation; the judicial review [55] process allows US judges to determine if actions taken by other branches of the government are unconstitutional.

          1.1.3.2.2.1. Pro:
          Judicial review of legislation can often have far-reaching effects, due to the interplay and overlap between different laws and jurisdictions (p. 5 [56]). Juries lack the comprehensive understanding required to make such determinations.

          1.1.3.2.2.2. Con:
          While the judiciary may only strike down laws at odds with the Constitution, juries may nullify [57] constitutional but nonetheless unfair laws. This can help address systemic issues in a way that the judiciary cannot.

          1.1.3.2.2.3. Con:
          In the same way that judicial review often acts as a check by ruling contrary [58] to the actions of other government branches, constitutional juries [59] could limit government power by reviewing laws and maintaining consensual community values.

        1.1.3.2.3. Pro:
        Jurors have been arbitrarily elevated to a position of power (p. 1 [60]); therefore, they cannot legitimately impose their will on the rest of the population (pp. 51-52 [61]).

      1.1.3.3. Pro:
      There are cases in which someone has committed a crime, but there are justifying or exempting [62] circumstances that would make it wrong to punish them as a criminal. The ability of juries to acquit people in such cases is essential.

        1.1.3.3.1. Con:
        Justifying or exempting circumstances and unjust laws should be incorporated into the law and rectified by democratic mechanisms that involve the entire electorate, not by the opinions of twelve laypersons.

          1.1.3.3.1.1. Pro:
          Lawyers can exploit ambiguous laws by portraying circumstances in a way that appears to justify, exempt, or mitigate a crime (p. 35 [63]). This does not promote just verdicts.

          1.1.3.3.1.2. Con:
          Many justifying and exempting circumstances [64] are codified in law; however, legislation cannot foresee the nuances of each case or identify whether the conditions have been met in the same way that juries can.

        1.1.3.3.2. Pro:
        Juries can acquit defendants for a number of exempting conditions or states, such as insanity or uncontrollable fear [65], that remove culpability for otherwise criminal acts.

        1.1.3.3.3. Pro:
        A jury can acquit the defendant for criminal actions that are legally or morally justified, such as homicide in the case of self-defense [66].

        1.1.3.3.4. Con:
        Judges are equally capable of acquitting defendants based on justifying or exempting circumstances [67].

      1.1.3.4. Con:
      Judges who have a moral objection to a particular law can effectively nullify the law [68] in the interest of justice.

        1.1.3.4.1. Con:
        Judges making such a ruling in the face of substantial evidence contrary to their decision would likely be subject to investigation [69] for misconduct.

        1.1.3.4.2. Con:
        US judges swear an oath [70] to uphold the nation's Constitution and laws.

          1.1.3.4.2.1. Con:
          The oath [71] charges US judges with "doing equal right" to all defendants; this can plausibly include refusing to uphold morally unjust laws.

        1.1.3.4.3. Pro:
        Judges have been known to acquit defendants when they disagree with the mandatory minimum sentence [72] for a crime.

    1.1.4. Con:
    Juries are not necessarily better than judges at checking the power [73] of the government.

      1.1.4.1. Pro:
      US judges have powers that enable them to check and balance other branches of the government in ways that juries cannot.

        1.1.4.1.1. Pro:
        Unlike juries, judges have the power to make law under common law [74] jurisdictions, so they can more actively check government power.

        1.1.4.1.2. Con:
        Both federal [75] and state [76] judges are themselves part of the government [77], and therefore cannot act as an outside check on government power in the way that juries can.

      1.1.4.2. Pro:
      Most countries have rigorous systems to ensure that judges remain independent; judicial independence allows judges to make fair decisions based only on the law and its application to presented evidence (p. 2 [78]).

        1.1.4.2.1. Con:
        In many developing countries, maintaining judicial independence is nearly impossible (pp. 1-2 [79]); in these countries, a jury-based system would be more independent.

          1.1.4.2.1.1. Pro:
          The less-established judicial systems of developing countries are more conducive to corruption (pp. 4-5 [80]).

          1.1.4.2.1.2. Pro:
          Three-quarters of Moroccan citizens agreed that bribing judges produces favorable outcomes [81].

          1.1.4.2.1.3. Con:
          Logistics planning in many developing countries is a prominent and troublesome issue (p. 348 [82]); reliably getting the members of the jury to court on time every day could prove to be a significant problem.

        1.1.4.2.2. Pro:
        The UN has established a number of basic principles for the establishment and protection [83] of judicial independence.

      1.1.4.3. Con:
      The executive and legislative branches in several EU countries are beginning to exert increased influence over the judiciary [84].

        1.1.4.3.1. Con:
        The European Union has a council and court particularly dedicated to maintaining the judicial independence of member states (p. 1 [85]).

        1.1.4.3.2. Pro:
        The expansion of jury trials, which are currently only used in about half of the EU member states, could help curtail government influence over the justice system (p. 110 [86]).

      1.1.4.4. Con:
      In India and the UK, judges can legally receive post-retirement benefits or positions [87] from the government, which can create bias and make judges less likely to check government power.

        1.1.4.4.1. Pro:
        In India, judges who deliver a verdict in favor of the government are 13-17% more likely to be appointed [88] to a post-judicial position; this severely undermines judicial independence.

    1.1.5. Pro:
    A lack of oversight and supervision [89] of the judiciary has often led to rampant corruption and misconduct (pp. 11-12 [90]); juries are not associated with such issues.

      1.1.5.1. Pro:
      Judges who preside over a large number of similar cases can be corrupted by institutional interests [91] related to their field of law.

        1.1.5.1.1. Pro:
        An Indonesian judge was imprisoned for taking bribes from a meat importer in order to make animal husbandry laws more favorable [92].

        1.1.5.1.2. Pro:
        In 2008, two US judges pleaded guilty to accepting millions of dollars in bribes [93] from a private youth detention facility in exchange for imposing harsher sentences on juveniles who appeared in their courts.

        1.1.5.1.3. Con:
        Judges from a variety of countries have been known to fight corruption from the inside by denouncing their corrupt colleagues [94].

      1.1.5.2. Pro:
      Bribing one judge [95] is easier and far less risky than bribing every member of the jury.

        1.1.5.2.1. Con:
        The attorney in question was an informant for the FBI and helped arrest [96] a judge in Texas for taking bribes, demonstrating that bribing judges is neither easy nor risk-free.

        1.1.5.2.2. Con:
        Most judges earn over $200,000 [97] per year, which makes them more difficult to bribe than the majority of jurors.

          1.1.5.2.2.1. Con:
          Many wealthy people around the world have amassed their wealth through corruption and ill-begotten gains [98], so the fact that judges are wealthy does not make them less likely to be corrupt.

          1.1.5.2.2.2. Pro:
          Judges are held to a higher standard [99] than unscrupulous businesspeople.

          1.1.5.2.2.3. Pro:
          Average personal income in the US is about $63,000 per year [100], suggesting that bribing a juror would be easier than bribing a judge.

        1.1.5.2.3. Pro:
        Lawyers often have existing relationships with judges; it is far more expensive, time consuming, and risky to track down and bribe a larger group of strangers [101].

        1.1.5.2.4. Con:
        Most developed countries have strict regulations in place that prevent judges from accepting bribes (p. 17 [102]).

          1.1.5.2.4.1. Con:
          Corruption could be easier to cover up in well-functioning and impartial judicial systems.

            1.1.5.2.4.1.1. Con:
            Functional and just legal systems have inbuilt characteristics and safeguards that inherently make judicial corruption more difficult (p. 90 [103]).

            1.1.5.2.4.1.2. Pro:
            Federal and state judges in the US receive an estimated [104] $2.5 million in bribes per year.

          1.1.5.2.4.2. Pro:
          A judge in Beijing was sentenced to four years in prison for accepting bribes from criminal gangs [105] to help them evade justice for their crimes.

          1.1.5.2.4.3. Pro:
          Most US states have established judicial commissions [106] specifically devoted to overseeing and investigating judiciary conduct; judges who are caught accepting bribes [107] face impeachment by legislators, censure by judicial councils, and criminal prosecution.

            1.1.5.2.4.3.1. Pro:
            In Arkansas, a state judge was sentenced to 10 years in prison [108] for accepting a bribe to reduce the amount of damages awarded in a civil case.

          1.1.5.2.4.4. Con:
          With or without regulations, judicial bribes are surprisingly common [109] in many developed countries.

      1.1.5.3. Con:
      More than 90% of state judicial misconduct complaints are dismissed every year, as these allegations amount to misunderstandings and legal errors, rather than actual misconduct (pp. 1246-1247 [110]).

        1.1.5.3.1. Pro:
        Common legal errors are sufficiently addressed by the appeals system and do not constitute judicial misconduct (pp. 68-69 [111]).

        1.1.5.3.2. Con:
        The bulk of ethics complaints against judges for unprofessional and inappropriate behavior are dismissed [112] without investigation.

      1.1.5.4. Pro:
      Many judges who have broken their oaths [113] remain on the bench and continue to victimize those in their constituency.

        1.1.5.4.1. Con:
        Judges who break their oaths by committing a crime are not immune to criminal liability [114].

        1.1.5.4.2. Pro:
        When witnesses break their oaths while on the stand, they commit perjury [115]; at a minimum, judges should be held to the same standard.

        1.1.5.4.3. Pro:
        Even if judges are removed due to misconduct, they have immunity [116] from civil lawsuits and can't be sued for monetary damages. This allows them to remain above the law and avoid the full repercussions of their misdeeds.

      1.1.5.5. Con:
      Jury misconduct cases are common [117].

        1.1.5.5.1. Pro:
        The omnipresence of social media and mobile internet access has led to a dramatic increase in internet-related misconduct by jurors (pp. 307-308 [118]).

        1.1.5.5.2. Con:
        A great deal of jury misconduct is accidental or harmless [119]; in many cases, a juror need only be issued a warning.

          1.1.5.5.2.1. Con:
          Some cases of jury misconduct result in acquittal of the guilty or conviction of the innocent (p. 4 [120]).

    1.1.6. Con:
    The ability of juries to check and balance government power is undermined in jurisdictions where prosecutors influence the composition of juries (p. 237 [121]).

      1.1.6.1. Pro:
      The Sixth Amendment [122] right to trial by jury in the US is intended to protect defendants from undue government influence. Prosecutorial participation [123] in jury selection is at odds with this goal (p. 1059 [124]).

        1.1.6.1.1. Pro:
        Government prosecutors have unfairly influenced the composition of juries [125] to more easily achieve convictions.

        1.1.6.1.2. Con:
        Since potential jurors, and their ability to consider a case impartially, are screened [126] by both the prosecution and the defense during the process of voir dire, juries are less susceptible to bias or government influence (p. 928 [127]).

          1.1.6.1.2.1. Pro:
          Expanding voir dire has been touted as a possible method of improving jury trials and reducing bias (p. 3 [128]).

            1.1.6.1.2.1.1. Con:
            Expanding voir dire [129] could make the jury selection process even more cumbersome and lengthy, while excluding too many people from being able to serve.

            1.1.6.1.2.1.2. Pro:
            Expanding voir dire in serious cases, such as those involving capital punishment, is necessary in order to ensure that jurors are truly qualified to serve (pp. 1211-1212 [130]).

          1.1.6.1.2.2. Con:
          Potential jurors are often intentionally screened out [131] by prosecutors and defense prior to trial, which can lead to biased juries.

            1.1.6.1.2.2.1. Pro:
            The selection of jurors can be political, as lawyers can seek to have potential jurors dismissed [132] if they believe their inclusion will not best serve their client.

          1.1.6.1.2.3. Con:
          Some lawyers argue [133] that 85% of cases litigated are won or lost when the jury is selected, which implies that lawyers seek to exploit the existing biases of jurors.

            1.1.6.1.2.3.1. Pro:
            In many US states, both parties in a legal proceeding can use a peremptory challenge [134] to remove a set number of problematic jurors without giving any justification.

      1.1.6.2. Con:
      The role of the jury as a check against government power is only symbolically undermined by the participation of the government in jury selection. There is no reason for a juror to become impartial in favor of the government simply by virtue of being selected.

        1.1.6.2.1. Con:
        Prosecutors can use leading questions and loaded language during jury selection to unfairly frame a case and affect jurors' interpretations of the evidence (pp. 17-18 [135]).

        1.1.6.2.2. Con:
        Prosecutors do not need to undermine the impartiality of jurors, they need only support the selection of jurors with an existing bias [136] that makes conviction more likely.

          1.1.6.2.2.1. Con:
          Prosecutors are forbidden from questioning potential jurors about a number of their personal opinions [137], which limits the ability to identify existing biases.

          1.1.6.2.2.2. Pro:
          The US has had many instances of prosecutors removing Black jurors [138] on the basis of their race.

            1.1.6.2.2.2.1. Pro:
            In the US, all-white juries disproportionately convict Black defendants [139] more often than juries including Black jurors.

        1.1.6.2.3. Pro:
        The primary concern of both the prosecution and defense during jury selection is establishing that the jurors can rule fairly; potential jurors can be excused if there is reasonable doubt as to their impartiality (p. 2 [140]).

  1.2. Con:
  Juries increase the legitimacy of the criminal justice system through the inclusion of the public in trial processes.

    1.2.1. Pro:
    Juries inject a community norm into the justice system, which maintains the necessary connection between the body politic and the judiciary (pp. 1141-1142, 1182 [141]).

      1.2.1.1. Pro:
      Criminal law [142] is intended to be a codification of the values and principles of society. Juries help ensure that laws are applied in line with these norms.

        1.2.1.1.1. Con:
        Law should be based upon human [143] and natural [144] rights; when community or society values are at odds with these, the use of juries detracts from the goal of promoting justice.

        1.2.1.1.2. Pro:
        Criminal law often conflicts with social values [145]; juries make it more difficult to enforce such laws.

        1.2.1.1.3. Pro:
        Although there are only six US states that still use jury sentencing, some believe the practice should be widely reinstated, as juries increase the legitimacy of sentencing by representing the community's standards (p. 353 [146]).

      1.2.1.2. Pro:
      The judiciary is largely composed of officials who were not elected, making them vulnerable to accusations that they are too far removed from community values [147]. Juries help remedy this through direct citizen involvement.

        1.2.1.2.1. Pro:
        Randomly selected [148] juries represent the community in a way comparable to democratic elections.

          1.2.1.2.1.1. Pro:
          Juries serve a similar purpose to elections by approximating the underlying preferences and beliefs of the population.

          1.2.1.2.1.2. Con:
          The jurors in the pool may be randomly selected, but the jurors who actually serve on a trial are selected by the prosecution and defense.

        1.2.1.2.2. Con:
        In many cases, judges are members of the local community [149] and have been educated, trained, and held other positions in the same district over which they preside.

        1.2.1.2.3. Pro:
        In the US, a group of randomly selected jurors is necessarily more representative of the wider population than a single judge, who is likely to be a white man (p. 9 [150]).

          1.2.1.2.3.1. Con:
          Twelve people is too small of a sample to be reliably representative of the wider population (p. 5 [151]).

          1.2.1.2.3.2. Pro:
          Many judges are millionaires or have amassed extensive wealth [152], so their perspectives are inherently disconnected from those of the average American.

          1.2.1.2.3.3. Pro:
          The gender and racial makeup of judges on state supreme courts fails to reflect [153] the increasingly diverse population.

          1.2.1.2.3.4. Con:
          People of color are chronically underrepresented [154] in American juries.

        1.2.1.2.4. Con:
        Economic factors have led to a disproportionate number of retired and unemployed people [155] serving on juries; as such, they may not represent an accurate cross section of a community's values.

          1.2.1.2.4.1. Con:
          The prosecution and defense can account for this imbalance by intentionally striking [156] the majority of unemployed people and retirees.

            1.2.1.2.4.1.1. Con:
            Age or employment status alone are insufficient reasons for dismissing a juror; such dismissals can be challenged (p. 77 [157]).

            1.2.1.2.4.1.2. Pro:
            Heterogeneous juries [158] deliberate more carefully and produce better verdicts.

          1.2.1.2.4.2. Pro:
          Criminal defense lawyers prefer unemployed people [159] and would instead strike employed people from jury consideration.

          1.2.1.2.4.3. Pro:
          Older juries are more likely to convict than younger juries (p. 1001 [160]).

      1.2.1.3. Con:
      Prioritizing public sentiment can produce problematic rulings or unjust outcomes.

        1.2.1.3.1. Pro:
        Reliance on public opinion in determining guilt and meting out punishment can lead to mob justice [161].

        1.2.1.3.2. Con:
        In cases where the law is clear and the jury has not voted in accordance with the law, a judge has the power to overturn [162] a jury's decision, change a sentence, or deliver a directed verdict.

          1.2.1.3.2.1. Con:
          Judges rarely overturn [163] jury trial decisions, as the necessary conditions for them to do so are uncommon.

          1.2.1.3.2.2. Pro:
          Judges have overturned [164] jury rulings in cases involving high-profile figures.

        1.2.1.3.3. Con:
        A functioning democracy should inherently prioritize the will of the people [165].

          1.2.1.3.3.1. Con:
          In the overwhelming majority of trials, public awareness of the case is nearly nonexistent [166] and limited to interested parties, so the will of the majority is irrelevant.

          1.2.1.3.3.2. Pro:
          Democratic laws are passed by either the people or elected representatives, so the very laws under which lawsuits or charges are brought are based on the will of the electorate.

          1.2.1.3.3.3. Pro:
          The decision of whether or not to bring charges is often in the hands of a democratically elected official, such as a district attorney [167].

        1.2.1.3.4. Pro:
        The will of the people may not be uniform; this can result in unjust outcomes for minority populations [168].

          1.2.1.3.4.1. Con:
          Race salience [169] causes jurors to pay extra attention to racial bias during racially-charged trials; this has been shown to lessen the influence of a defendant's race on trial outcomes.

          1.2.1.3.4.2. Pro:
          Laws that enforce segregation [170] seek to disenfranchise minority groups.

      1.2.1.4. Con:
      Cases decided by a jury are capable of being overturned [171] by a higher, non-jury court; this eliminates the connection to the people.

        1.2.1.4.1. Con:
        In France, an appeals tribunal includes twelve lay jury members (p. 462 [172]).

        1.2.1.4.2. Pro:
        US appeals courts do not use juries, since they do not hear new evidence and are focused on reviewing the fairness of the processes [173] followed during the original trial.

    1.2.2. Con:
    Juries operate with an unreasonable amount of secrecy [174], which decreases trust in the criminal justice system.

      1.2.2.1. Pro:
      Grand juries operate in near-total secrecy [175]; they are barred from disclosing any matter presented to the jury and all records are typically sealed. This is unreasonable and does not promote trust in the justice system.

        1.2.2.1.1. Con:
        The secrecy of grand juries is necessary to uphold the integrity of the indictment process and protect the rights of those under investigation (p. 1 [176]).

        1.2.2.1.2. Pro:
        Grand juries have often been found to return indictments based upon evidence that has been unconstitutionally obtained, yet the defense is prevented from accessing such records by the secrecy requirements (p. 181 [177]).

      1.2.2.2. Con:
      The nature of some crimes can require that such secrecy is maintained or even expanded to include anonymous juries (pp. 10-11 [178]).

        1.2.2.2.1. Pro:
        Cases involving organized crime or terrorism often use anonymous juries, as they are considered to be likely targets for jury tampering and intimidation (p. 15 [179]).

        1.2.2.2.2. Con:
        Anonymous juries are at odds with a defendant's Sixth Amendment right to a public trial (p. 1009 [180]).

        1.2.2.2.3. Pro:
        In high-profile cases, juror identities may be sealed [181] by the judge in order to protect their privacy.

      1.2.2.3. Con:
      Juries force lawyers to speak in clear, understandable language instead of legal jargon. This ensures the justice system remains transparent and accessible to defendants and the wider public.

        1.2.2.3.1. Pro:
        The need for clear, understandable language in legal proceedings and documents has garnered a great deal of support from the plain language movement (pp. 25-26 [182]).

        1.2.2.3.2. Con:
        Expert witnesses often make use of specialized jargon [183] from their fields; this can often leave jurors confused about the evidence that is being presented.

      1.2.2.4. Pro:
      The transparency, quality, and legitimacy of the court system are undermined by not requiring juries to provide reasons for their verdicts (p. 352 [184]).

        1.2.2.4.1. Pro:
        Without an explanation for how a verdict was reached, there is no reason to believe that deliberations were conducted in an impartial manner [185].

          1.2.2.4.1.1. Pro:
          Adverse court decisions can feel unfair [186] without knowing how and why they were made.

          1.2.2.4.1.2. Pro:
          Bias and emotional reactions [187] are rampant in jury deliberations.

          1.2.2.4.1.3. Con:
          A court case involves the presentation of arguments for both sides, and juries can only make a decision based on the evidence [188] presented in court.

            1.2.2.4.1.3.1. Con:
            Analysis of the language used by trial lawyers reveals that stylized language, and pathos in particular, predominates over consideration of the evidence (p. 93 [189]). This suggests that lawyers consider managing a jury's perception of the evidence to be more important than the evidence itself.

            1.2.2.4.1.3.2. Con:
            It is often possible to interpret evidence in many ways (p. 1159 [190]).

            1.2.2.4.1.3.3. Pro:
            Since verdicts are based on the evidence, the winning side is always the one that presented stronger evidence.

        1.2.2.4.2. Con:
        Rather than abolishing juries, the transparency of jury verdicts can be improved by the inclusion of a statement explaining the jury's reasoning.

          1.2.2.4.2.1. Pro:
          In some countries, juries are required to give reasons for their verdict (p. 620 [191]).

          1.2.2.4.2.2. Con:
          Countries that require juries to provide reasons for their decisions are the exception, not the norm (pp. 2, 11 [192]).

        1.2.2.4.3. Con:
        In order to protect jurors, the US Supreme Court unanimously ruled that the members of a jury are prohibited from testifying [193] in court about their deliberations.

          1.2.2.4.3.1. Pro:
          If juries were recorded or had to provide their reasons, they would likely be deterred from speaking with candor.

            1.2.2.4.3.1.1. Con:
            Juries could be required to provide a short written justification for the group's decision that keeps each individual juror's opinion anonymous.

          1.2.2.4.3.2. Con:
          Requiring jurors to provide reasons for their decisions could force them to deliberate more critically rather than relying on unchallenged assumptions and groupthink [194].

        1.2.2.4.4. Pro:
        An explanation of the rationale behind a verdict is necessary to support the rehabilitative aspects [195] of the criminal justice system.

          1.2.2.4.4.1. Con:
          Rehabilitation is not the primary concern [196] of the criminal justice system, nor should it be [197].

          1.2.2.4.4.2. Pro:
          There is strong evidence that increasing the rehabilitative elements of a justice system can lower the rate of recidivism [198].

          1.2.2.4.4.3. Con:
          Rehabilitative justice is overly concerned with the needs of offenders; in comparison, restorative justice [199] promotes greater accountability and concern for the welfare of those who have been harmed and the community at large.

          1.2.2.4.4.4. Pro:
          Without such an explanation, convicted defendants are likely to feel wronged by the justice system, which can alienate them from society.

            1.2.2.4.4.4.1. Con:
            The potential alienation of convicted persons is not within the purview of the criminal justice system.

            1.2.2.4.4.4.2. Pro:
            A lack of explanation for a verdict may lead a convict to assume it was made arbitrarily and leave them feeling unfairly treated and unable to move on.

              1.2.2.4.4.4.2.1. Pro:
              It may be important to the defendant to know which pieces of evidence or arguments a jury found most convincing [200], which is often unclear.

              1.2.2.4.4.4.2.2. Con:
              Only wrongfully convicted defendants would legitimately feel alienated by a lack of explanation.

        1.2.2.4.5. Con:
        If there are concerns about the fairness [201] of a verdict, then jury secrecy is not always maintained.

          1.2.2.4.5.1. Con:
          Judges are strongly motivated to protect the secrecy of jury deliberations and juror anonymity wherever possible (p. 208 [202]).

          1.2.2.4.5.2. Pro:
          The US Supreme Court has ruled that if there is evidence of racism during jury deliberations, a judge is obligated to make an exception [203] to jury secrecy to ensure that the defendant was fairly tried.

            1.2.2.4.5.2.1. Con:
            This ruling has not been consistently enforced [204] by lower courts, often allowing racial bias to continue unchecked.

        1.2.2.4.6. Pro:
        In cases where prominent defendants are controversially acquitted or convicted, informing the public about the reasons for the decision is crucial.

          1.2.2.4.6.1. Con:
          Numerous high-profile cases have been televised, allowing the public to follow the process and testimony themselves (p. 891 [205]).

            1.2.2.4.6.1.1. Con:
            Televised trials can make it almost impossible to find impartial jurors [206]; this undermines the fairness of a trial.

          1.2.2.4.6.2. Con:
          Even in high-profile cases, public opinion is secondary [207] to the verdict reached by the jury based on the evidence presented by the prosecution.

          1.2.2.4.6.3. Pro:
          High-profile cases can lead to widespread public backlash, often directed at jurors themselves (p. 936 [208]). Drawing out the salient reasons for a verdict promotes the view that the jurors are deeply concerned with the rightness of their decision, offsetting any public backlash.

      1.2.2.5. Con:
      Jury members are allowed to speak to the press [209] about their decisions once the trial has concluded.

        1.2.2.5.1. Con:
        Jury members are not required to speak to the press and many decline to do so.

        1.2.2.5.2. Pro:
        Providing the press with details about a decided criminal case supports the public's First Amendment right to information about public affairs (p. 836 [210]).

        1.2.2.5.3. Con:
        In the UK, jurors are prohibited from speaking to the media [211] during or following the conclusion of a case.

    1.2.3. Pro:
    Jury trials promote the legitimacy of the justice system via democratic participation (p. 1132 [212]).

      1.2.3.1. Con:
      Many people actively attempt to avoid being selected for jury duty (pp. 2, 5 [213]); therefore, direct personal participation is not necessary to support judicial legitimacy.

        1.2.3.1.1. Con:
        People who believe that serving on a jury is their civic duty [214] do not actively attempt to avoid being selected.

          1.2.3.1.1.1. Pro:
          Older and more educated people are more likely to view jury duty as part of being an upstanding citizen [215].

        1.2.3.1.2. Pro:
        Although many people do not vote [216], most still consider the results of elections to be legitimate.

        1.2.3.1.3. Pro:
        Even people who avoid jury service generally uphold the legitimacy of the justice system and its verdicts.

          1.2.3.1.3.1. Pro:
          Two-thirds of the people who are called to serve manage to get out of jury duty [217], yet there is not broad disagreement over court legitimacy.

          1.2.3.1.3.2. Con:
          A lack of participation can signal issues surrounding legitimacy. For example, in minority and marginalized communities, biased policing and sentencing practices have contributed to perceptions of the justice system as illegitimate (p. 15 [218]).

          1.2.3.1.3.3. Pro:
          Although there has been a decrease in recent years, 58% percent of surveyed Americans [219] still express having some, a lot, or a great deal of confidence in the US legal system.

        1.2.3.1.4. Con:
        Allowing fewer excuses for not participating in jury duty would increase participation (p. 1 [220]).

          1.2.3.1.4.1. Con:
          Allowing fewer excuses could place an excessive burden on jurors with substantial outside obligations.

          1.2.3.1.4.2. Pro:
          Although there are penalties for skipping jury duty, very few people are actually fined [221] for not showing up. Enforcing the fines would encourage people to participate.

        1.2.3.1.5. Con:
        Jury trials should still be held, but jury service should be voluntary [222].

          1.2.3.1.5.1. Con:
          Voluntary jury service would increase the amount of bias [223] in demographically homogeneous areas.

          1.2.3.1.5.2. Con:
          If jury service were voluntary, some trials might not be able to move forward due to a lack of volunteers.

          1.2.3.1.5.3. Pro:
          Voluntary jury service would grant people who are enthusiastic and excited to become a juror the opportunity to serve.

      1.2.3.2. Con:
      The process for assembling juries can be biased against certain groups, preventing them from participating equally in the justice system.

        1.2.3.2.1. Pro:
        The racial makeup of a jury often does not reflect a true sample [224] of the defendant's peers.

          1.2.3.2.1.1. Pro:
          Black and Latinx jurors with felony records are systematically excluded [225] from serving as jurors, further reducing the likelihood that a given jury is representative of the defendant's peers.

          1.2.3.2.1.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.1.6.2.2.2.

          1.2.3.2.1.3. Con:
          Criminal justice experts have proposed numerous methods for reducing racial bias in jury selection (p. 99 [226]).

            1.2.3.2.1.3.1. Con:
            There is no simple fix to rectify anti-minority bias in juries; this would require years of sustained and difficult work to decrease racism in society at large.

            1.2.3.2.1.3.2. Pro:
            Anti-minority bias in jury selection is often caused by structural [227] and societal [228] issues unrelated to the jury selection process itself. These can be rectified without abolishing juries altogether.

            1.2.3.2.1.3.3. Pro:
            Some have argued that peremptory challenges [229] must be removed in order to rectify racial bias in jury selection (p. 385 [230]).

              1.2.3.2.1.3.3.1. Pro:
              Peremptory challenges are unable to reduce attorney bias and can even exacerbate some biases (p. 467 [231]).

              1.2.3.2.1.3.3.2. Con:
              Racial bias in peremptory challenges occurs for both white and Black prospective jurors; the defense is four times more likely [232] to remove white people through peremptory challenges.

          1.2.3.2.1.4. Pro:
          Ethnic minorities are underrepresented on juries in Australia and New Zealand, partially as a result of discriminatory challenges by prosecutors (p. 49 [233]).

        1.2.3.2.2. Pro:
        Age-related discrimination is not protected by the US Constitution, which allows the prosecution or defense to dismiss jurors based on their age (p. 206 [234]).

        1.2.3.2.3. Con:
        In the US, race and gender-based discrimination during jury selection are prohibited by the Constitution (p. 210 [235]).

        1.2.3.2.4. Con:
        If the justice system of a particular area is sufficiently prejudiced to allow biased juries, then the judges in that system are likely prejudiced as well and would not be an improvement over juries (pp. 1114-1115 [236]).

          1.2.3.2.4.1. Con:
          Judges are highly educated [237] and not necessarily from the area over which they preside; as such, they are less likely than the average citizen to conform to local prejudices.

            1.2.3.2.4.1.1. Con:
            Judges from other areas may hold prejudices about the locale where they are appointed (p. V [238]). A lack of understanding of local customs and culture could lead to major faux pas when presiding over cases with locals.

            1.2.3.2.4.1.2. Pro:
            The relocation of highly-educated judges can bring knowledge and expertise to regions lacking in both (pp. 351-352 [239]).

          1.2.3.2.4.2. Pro:
          A number of studies have identified systemic racial bias [240] throughout the US justice system; if the system itself involves inherent bias, it matters little who is deciding a case.

      1.2.3.3. Con:
      Most Americans are too uninformed [241] to truly take part in the democratic process.

        1.2.3.3.1. Pro:
        The decline in the use of juries in the US is attributable to a lack of public confidence in the ability of juries to understand complex legal terms and situations (p. 1 [242]).

        1.2.3.3.2. Con:
        Even people who are uninformed about the democratic process can serve on a jury and meaningfully contribute to democracy.

        1.2.3.3.3. Pro:
        Since those who are incapable of meaningfully participating in democracy by virtue of being uninformed [243] can still act as jurors, jury trials do not promote democratic participation.

          1.2.3.3.3.1. Pro:
          There has been a noted increase in the preference for uninformed jurors [244] during jury selection.

      1.2.3.4. Pro:
      People are more likely to increase their civic participation [245] after their jury service is over.

        1.2.3.4.1. Con:
        People who are willing to perform jury duty may already have a higher propensity for civic duty compared with those who avoid being selected (p. 1 [246]).

        1.2.3.4.2. Pro:
        Civic participation is associated with improved health and well-being [247] and strengthens both existing and new social networks.

          1.2.3.4.2.1. Con:
          People who are more interested in civic participation may already have a higher propensity for healthy habits compared with those who avoid their civic duties. There are likely other confounding factors [248] as well.

        1.2.3.4.3. Pro:
        Compulsory jury service promotes community values and collectivism in citizens, who would otherwise focus solely on their individual rights and goals (p. 51 [249]).

        1.2.3.4.4. Con:
        Not all types of jury trials have the same effect; participation in civil jury service does not lead to an increase in civic engagement (p. 697 [250]).

      1.2.3.5. Pro:
      Without jury trials, public knowledge of the justice system would be primarily based on film and television [251] portrayals, which are often inaccurate. Inclusion of the public helps educate people about how courts actually function.

        1.2.3.5.1. Con:
        This is evidence that primary and secondary school education regarding the justice system needs to be improved and expanded upon (p. 32 [252]), not that the jury system itself should be preserved.

        1.2.3.5.2. Pro:
        Serving on a jury can help citizens to gain a better understanding [253] of the issues facing their community and the inner workings of the justice system itself.

        1.2.3.5.3. Pro:
        Voters cannot make informed decisions about the criminal justice system unless they understand how it functions (p. 555 [254]).

        1.2.3.5.4. Con:
        Annually, 0.02% [255] of US adults serve on federal jury trials [256], and 0.63% [257] serve on state jury trials. Such a minuscule proportion of the population does not have a meaningful impact on public knowledge of the justice system.

          1.2.3.5.4.1. Pro:
          From 2006 to 2016, the number of jurors called to serve by federal courts fell by 39% [258].

          1.2.3.5.4.2. Con:
          Despite being few in number, people who serve on a jury communicate with friends and family about their experiences and spread knowledge about the justice system throughout society.

      1.2.3.6. Pro:
      Jury service is the most significant manner in which citizens participate in the democratic process (p. 205 [259]).

        1.2.3.6.1. Pro:
        The political power of citizens is highly diffused [260] in a representative democracy; juries act as representatives of the public's interest.

        1.2.3.6.2. Con:
        The most important method by which citizens can participate in the democratic process is by voting.

          1.2.3.6.2.1. Con:
          Jury service is a more active form of democratic participation than voting, as jurors act directly rather than via elected representatives (p. 1035 [261]).

          1.2.3.6.2.2. Con:
          Both jury service and voting suffer from an inherent weakness of democracy: they are subject to the biases, lack of knowledge, and limited understanding of the populace (p. 1168 [262]).

          1.2.3.6.2.3. Con:
          Jury service is a more direct form of citizen participation, as voters have a minuscule chance of making a decisive difference in an election (p. 1170 [263]).

          1.2.3.6.2.4. Pro:
          There are ten times as many people registered to vote [264] in elections as there are jurors in state and federal trials [265].

          1.2.3.6.2.5. Pro:
          Elections often reshape the political and social landscape and lead to sweeping change [266] as newly elected candidates and parties implement new programs and policies.

    1.2.4. Con:
    At times, juries inconsistently or unjustly apply laws (pp. 27, 29 [267]). This reduces public faith in the justice system.

      1.2.4.1. Pro:
      Judges are expected to adhere to case law [268]. If they do not, they can be fired or reprimanded. Juries are not held to such a standard.

        1.2.4.1.1. Con:
        Judges must consider precedent in their rulings, but are not required to abide by it. An essential function of the judiciary is the reinterpretation [269] of case law.

        1.2.4.1.2. Pro:
        Jury verdicts are not required to be internally or externally consistent (p. 29 [270]).

        1.2.4.1.3. Con:
        Jury decisions that represent a notable departure from precedent [271] are often decided by appellate courts.

      1.2.4.2. Con:
      Juries support public accountability and the consent of the governed, which contribute to public trust and legitimacy (p. 1 [272]).

        1.2.4.2.1. Pro:
        If the judicial system is not perceived as legitimate, its rulings may not be respected or followed by the people or other branches of the government.

          1.2.4.2.1.1. Con:
          In the US, enforcement of the law is an executive function (p. 803 [273]). Thus, even if the judiciary were not perceived as legitimate by the public, the executive branch could compel people to adhere to its decisions.

          1.2.4.2.1.2. Pro:
          The judiciary does not have the power to enforce its rulings without the cooperation and support [274] of other branches of government.

        1.2.4.2.2. Con:
        In the absence of juries, the judicial system would still be perceived as legitimate so long as its processes were perceived to be fair and impartial.

          1.2.4.2.2.1. Con:
          Jury trials directly contribute to the legitimacy of democratic justice systems by supporting the idea that all reasonable citizens are equally capable of passing judgment (p. 2 [275]).

          1.2.4.2.2.2. Pro:
          If judicial processes and interactions [276] are perceived to be moral, honest, and trustworthy, then the justice system is legitimized.

      1.2.4.3. Con:
      To trust a jury is to place faith in the process [277] of democracy itself, particularly in cases where the defendant has been wrongfully accused.

        1.2.4.3.1. Con:
        Such faith may be unwarranted, given the high number of wrongful convictions [278].

        1.2.4.3.2. Pro:
        A belief in the capability of juries to arrive at a correct verdict requires trust in the intelligence, rationality, and empathy of one's fellow citizens [279].

  1.3. Pro:
  Jury trials are inefficient and inconvenient for both courts and jurors.

    1.3.1. Pro:
    Serving on a jury has a negative impact [280] on the lives of jurors because it significantly interrupts their routines.

      1.3.1.1. Pro:
      The dates and hours of jury duty are inflexible; this can conflict with jurors' other obligations.

        1.3.1.1.1. Pro:
        Jury duty may require parents to secure additional childcare [281].

        1.3.1.1.2. Con:
        In most cases, courts are flexible and offer people time to rearrange their private and professional schedules [282] to accommodate the requirements of serving as a juror.

        1.3.1.1.3. Con:
        In most courts, jury service can be postponed [283] one or more times to allow for personal circumstances.

        1.3.1.1.4. Pro:
        Jurors are typically only offered one alternative date [284] for jury duty; they must plan their entire lives around an event that may last for several days.

      1.3.1.2. Con:
      Most people have minor obligations [285] with regard to jury duty: half of the people called for service are never required to report; only 7% of those selected actually end up in a courtroom; and only 1% actually serve as jurors.

        1.3.1.2.1. Pro:
        Jurors cannot be called to serve more than once every two years [286].

        1.3.1.2.2. Con:
        In some cases, jury duty can last up to two years [287], which makes it nearly impossible for jurors to remain integrated in their normal lives.

          1.3.1.2.2.1. Con:
          Given that the average trial only lasts two to three days [288], this is not a substantial concern.

      1.3.1.3. Pro:
      Many people find serving on a jury to be boring [289].

        1.3.1.3.1. Con:
        Being bored is not sufficient justification to abolish a well-established practice.

        1.3.1.3.2. Con:
        Many people are bored in general [290] in their daily lives; some people might find jury duty to be a nice change of pace [291] compared with their normal life.

        1.3.1.3.3. Pro:
        Bored and inattentive jurors can make mistakes if they aren't paying attention to the details of a case (pp. 11, 16 [292]).

      1.3.1.4. Con:
      Serving on a jury is an important civic responsibility [293] for all citizens; the convenience of such practices is irrelevant.

        1.3.1.4.1. Pro:
        Two-thirds of Americans feel that jury duty is a civic responsibility of citizenship [294], so any inconvenience is a minor concern.

        1.3.1.4.2. Con:
        The government is meant to make things more, not less, convenient [295] for citizens.

      1.3.1.5. Pro:
      Jury duty has detrimental economic effects on jurors and businesses due to the amount of time jurors spend away from their jobs.

        1.3.1.5.1. Pro:
        Jurors can be prevented from working for up to 15 business days [296], which can have a substantial impact on company productivity.

          1.3.1.5.1.1. Con:
          The average trial lasts only two or three days [297], so in the majority of cases, the impact on productivity is minimal.

          1.3.1.5.1.2. Pro:
          Each juror results in a loss of $650 of company productivity per day (p. 17 [298]).

          1.3.1.5.1.3. Pro:
          Jurors who miss work in order to serve can fall behind, resulting in decreased productivity even after they return to their jobs.

        1.3.1.5.2. Con:
        Under US federal law, jurors are only obligated to serve for a few days [299] once every two years [300], suggesting that the economic effects of jury service are exaggerated.

        1.3.1.5.3. Con:
        Jury service has minimal effects on consumer spending.

          1.3.1.5.3.1. Con:
          Jurors are likely to spend less money during jury duty, as their time will be primarily occupied by the case and deliberations (p. 138 [301]).

          1.3.1.5.3.2. Pro:
          Since the hours of jury duty generally correspond with regular business hours [302], most jurors are likely to continue with their usual spending patterns while performing jury service.

        1.3.1.5.4. Pro:
        The financial impact of being a juror can have negative effects [303] on people's livelihoods.

          1.3.1.5.4.1. Con:
          Many employers continue, either voluntarily or by state mandate [304], to pay employees who are absent because of jury duty.

            1.3.1.5.4.1.1. Con:
            Employers are hit twice as hard by having to pay roughly $200, which is the median daily income [305] of Americans, on top of the lost productivity.

            1.3.1.5.4.1.2. Pro:
            Even in states where employers are not required to pay employees during jury service, federal law dictates that exempt employees [306] must continue to receive full pay unless they do not perform any work for a full week.

            1.3.1.5.4.1.3. Con:
            In Connecticut, employers are only required to pay their employees for the first five days of jury duty; after that, the state pays jurors $50/day (p. 2 [307]).

            1.3.1.5.4.1.4. Pro:
            Even the states that do not require employers to pay employees who are serving on a jury generally provide a number of protections [308] to such employees.

          1.3.1.5.4.2. Con:
          Jurors are paid [309] for jury duty and reimbursed for their expenses.

            1.3.1.5.4.2.1. Pro:
            Unemployed people would certainly welcome the opportunity to earn some income.

              1.3.1.5.4.2.1.1. Con:
              Some US states reduce unemployment benefits [310] by the amount of money earned during jury duty; there would be no increase in pay, making jury duty a waste of time.

              1.3.1.5.4.2.1.2. Pro:
              Jurors in the UK, though unpaid, can continue to receive unemployment benefits for the first eight weeks [311] of jury duty.

            1.3.1.5.4.2.2. Con:
            State juries pay far less than federal juries; for the first five days of a trial, California [312] pays just $15 a day and reimburses the cost of travel.

            1.3.1.5.4.2.3. Pro:
            Some cities are piloting programs that pay low-income jurors a higher daily rate [313].

            1.3.1.5.4.2.4. Pro:
            Dozens of states have adopted the Jury Patriotism Act, which allows jurors to request $100-$300 of supplemental compensation due to extenuating circumstances (p. 6 [314]).

            1.3.1.5.4.2.5. Con:
            Jurors in the UK are not paid [315].

            1.3.1.5.4.2.6. Con:
            Jurors are poorly compensated [316] for their service in comparison with what they would have received from their employer (pp. 16-17 [317]).

          1.3.1.5.4.3. Pro:
          In jobs where income is tied to commission [318] and sales volume, even a few days away from work can have a major impact on monthly income.

          1.3.1.5.4.4. Pro:
          Most of the US workforce [319] is employed in hourly service or manufacturing jobs; since these workers are paid hourly, their income is likely to be seriously impacted.

          1.3.1.5.4.5. Pro:
          Jurors who are students or small business owners [320] can suffer substantial financial damages if they are required to serve for an extended time.

          1.3.1.5.4.6. Con:
          Jurors in some jurisdictions can be excused from service due to financial hardship. [321]

            1.3.1.5.4.6.1. Con:
            Due to a shortage of jurors [322], some courts do not accept financial hardship as an excuse.

    1.3.2. Con:
    The purpose of a jury trial is to produce a just verdict, not to maximize convenience or efficiency (p. 3 [323]).

      1.3.2.1. Pro:
      The time and resources spent on assembling juries and conducting jury trials are justified if juries deliver better outcomes than judges.

        1.3.2.1.1. Con:
        One judicial outcome being better than another depends on a number of subjective and hard-to-measure variables (p. 4 [324]).

          1.3.2.1.1.1. Pro:
          Objectively evaluating the fairness of the outcome of a case is essentially impossible, since it presupposes absolute knowledge about the facts of a case.

          1.3.2.1.1.2. Con:
          The Measures for Justice project continues to develop methods for collecting and analyzing data on judicial outcomes across the US (p. 15 [325]).

        1.3.2.1.2. Pro:
        The purpose of the judicial system is successfully determining who is innocent and who is guilty [326]. If fewer innocent people are found guilty, then the additional time spent on juries is justified.

          1.3.2.1.2.1. Con:
          One study found that 80% of examined exonerations had been convicted by juries [327], whereas only 7% had been convicted by judges.

          1.3.2.1.2.2. Pro:
          The US legal system prioritizes protecting the innocent over punishing the guilty (pp. 413, 415 [328]).

        1.3.2.1.3. Pro:
        The quality of judicial system outcomes can be quantified in terms of the proportion of guilty verdicts which are subsequently overturned on appeal.

          1.3.2.1.3.1. Pro:
          The low rate of reversals for cases tried by judges may be symptomatic of bias in the appellate courts in favor of judicial decisions (p. 5 [329]).

          1.3.2.1.3.2. Con:
          Jury trial decisions are overturned upon appeal at a substantially higher rate than bench trial verdicts; jury trials therefore produce inferior outcomes (pp. 3, 5 [330]).

            1.3.2.1.3.2.1. Con:
            This is not an accurate measure of fairness, as many verdicts are overturned on procedural grounds [331] that have nothing to do with fairness.

      1.3.2.2. Con:
      Efforts to increase efficiency are not inherently at odds with the goal of producing fair outcomes; eliminating juries could conceivably serve both goals.

        1.3.2.2.1. Con:
        The additional deliberation that is required in jury trials necessarily supports just outcomes (p. 4 [332]); any attempt to streamline or reduce this process inherently undermines the fairness of verdicts.

        1.3.2.2.2. Pro:
        Given that jury decisions are reversed on appeal at a substantially higher rate than bench decisions, eliminating juries could simultaneously improve fairness and increase efficiency (p. 3 [333]).

      1.3.2.3. Pro:
      The decisions of the judicial system have an immense impact on people's lives [334]. This is more important than the financial cost incurred.

        1.3.2.3.1. Con:
        The parent claim presents a false dilemma [335]; the impact of judicial decisions and the cost of operating the judicial system are both important considerations.

        1.3.2.3.2. Pro:
        Criminal courts are empowered to punish people with prison or death sentences. These are gross violations of individual liberty and require a fair and just process (p. viii [336]).

      1.3.2.4. Con:
      Since it is questionable whether juries actually produce more just outcomes, it makes sense to focus on maximizing court efficiency by eliminating the jury system.

    1.3.3. Con:
    The inefficiency and inconvenience of juries are irrelevant, since the right to a fair trial [337] is a cornerstone of the legal system in most modern democracies.

      1.3.3.1. Pro:
      The Sixth and Seventh Amendments [338] established the right to a trial by jury in the US.

        1.3.3.1.1. Con:
        In nearly every country on Earth, civil jury trials—those protected by the Seventh Amendment—either never existed or have been abolished [339].

          1.3.3.1.1.1. Pro:
          The Seventh Amendment is irrelevant to most Americans; fewer than 1% of federal civil trials are decided by a jury [340].

        1.3.3.1.2. Con:
        The right to a civil jury trial can be overruled by invoking a summary judgment [341], where a judge dismisses a case prior to the jury trial due to a lack of evidence.

        1.3.3.1.3. Pro:
        The Founding Fathers considered trial by jury to be a necessary protection [342] against unfair debts, unjust laws, legal suits involving the government, and overreach by judges.

      1.3.3.2. Con:
      Trial by jury originated in England and was not widely adopted outside of the former British colonies; today, juries are primarily an American phenomenon [343].

        1.3.3.2.1. Con:
        The British refusal to allow American participation in colonial jury trials led to the enshrinement of jury trials [344] in the US Constitution as a valuable element of self-government.

        1.3.3.2.2. Pro:
        American legal influence is waning (p. 421 [345]); fewer international courts are paying attention to the decisions of the US Supreme Court.

          1.3.3.2.2.1. Pro:
          The US Supreme Court overturned existing protections on the right to abortion [346] at a time when many other countries were decriminalizing or legalizing it, demonstrating that US judicial influence is on the decline.

        1.3.3.2.3. Con:
        The US Constitution, which forms the basis of the US legal system, has influenced the constitutions of most of the world's democracies (p. 1 [347]).

          1.3.3.2.3.1. Con:
          Not every country that used the US Constitution as the basis for their own constitution [348] has instituted jury trials.

      1.3.3.3. Pro:
      A number of countries have recently started to incorporate jury trials to better democratize their criminal justice system, despite their inconvenience (p. 276 [349]).

        1.3.3.3.1. Pro:
        After abolishing trial by jury in 1943, Japan [350] reinstated the practice in 2009 in order to "hail a new era of openness."

        1.3.3.3.2. Pro:
        In 2008, South Korea [351] held its first trial by jury in an effort to modernize its justice system by incorporating aspects of the US legal system.

        1.3.3.3.3. Con:
        The decision to implement a jury system is not always democratic [352]; its purpose is often to lessen the power of judges who were appointed by opposing or previous governments.

        1.3.3.3.4. Con:
        Other countries are reducing or eliminating [353] the use of jury trials.

          1.3.3.3.4.1. Pro:
          India abolished jury trials [354] in 1973 after finding them to be an ineffective method of arriving at a just verdict.

      1.3.3.4. Pro:
      The Universal Declaration of Human Rights identifies "a fair and public hearing by an independent and impartial tribunal" as a basic human right (p. 3 [355]).

        1.3.3.4.1. Pro:
        Per the Third and Fourth Geneva Convention, denying a person the right to a fair and impartial trial can constitute a war crime (p. 269 [356]).

        1.3.3.4.2. Con:
        The Universal Declaration of Human Rights is not a binding legal agreement [357] for many countries.

      1.3.3.5. Con:
      Many countries, though proponents of democracy, do not employ jury trials (pp. 372-373 [358]).

        1.3.3.5.1. Con:
        Though juries are uncommon in Belgian courts, they still exist for specific purposes [359], suggesting that there is no single best way for European countries to organize their judicial systems.

        1.3.3.5.2. Pro:
        Judges, not juries, preside over all court hearings in Nordic countries [360].

        1.3.3.5.3. Pro:
        Germany uses a lay assessor [361] court system composed of three professional judges and two or three lay assessors instead of a jury system.

        1.3.3.5.4. Pro:
        According to the European Court and the European Commission of Human Rights, defendants do not have a right to a trial by jury (p. 7 [362]).

          1.3.3.5.4.1. Con:
          The European Court is not the gold standard for judicial practices around the world (p. 242 [363]).

        1.3.3.5.5. Con:
        In the 1990s, Spain and Russia reintroduced the jury trial system, which expanded the democratic elements of society and increased the protection of defendant rights (p. 233 [364]).

          1.3.3.5.5.1. Con:
          The Russian government has a history of not respecting the decisions [365] of national court judgements.

      1.3.3.6. Con:
      Jury systems don't necessarily increase the fairness of trials or promote democracy.

        1.3.3.6.1. Pro:
        Proponents of modern democracy, such as Montesquieu and de Tocqueville, have rallied against the idea of popular juries (p. 52 [366]).

        1.3.3.6.2. Pro:
        Citizen juries would be more likely to promote fair trials in an epistocracy [367] than in a democracy.

        1.3.3.6.3. Con:
        Judges, prosecutors, and defense attorneys all rank juries as fairer than bench trials in criminal cases, despite being slower and less cost-effective (p. 141 [368]).

    1.3.4. Pro:
    A significant amount of time and resources are required to assemble and operate juries (pp. 2-3 [369]).

      1.3.4.1. Pro:
      Jury trials take more time than cases tried by judges.

        1.3.4.1.1. Pro:
        On average, jury trials take about twice as long [370] as trials decided by a judge.

          1.3.4.1.1.1. Con:
          -> See 1.3.1.2.2.1.

          1.3.4.1.1.2. Pro:
          In cases where there are few witnesses, bench trials can often be decided in about three hours [371].

          1.3.4.1.1.3. Pro:
          Since bench trials dispose of cases more quickly, they result in less backlog [372] within the judicial system.

        1.3.4.1.2. Con:
        Expedited jury trials typically take just one day [373] for the jurors to reach a verdict.

        1.3.4.1.3. Pro:
        Jury trials in the UK typically last for two weeks [374], whereas cases heard by the Magistrate's Court usually last five to six hours [375].

        1.3.4.1.4. Con:
        Jury trials in California typically last just one day (p. 1 [376]).

        1.3.4.1.5. Con:
        Judicial backlogs [377] incentivize judges to turn out decisions as quickly as possible; this works contrary to duly considering each case.

          1.3.4.1.5.1. Con:
          Jurors are often exposed to pressure from judges and other jurors [378] to quickly arrive at a decision, which undermines their consideration of a verdict.

          1.3.4.1.5.2. Pro:
          Pressure on judges to quickly decide cases is likely to increase, as court backlog intensified on a global scale [379] due to the COVID-19 pandemic.

            1.3.4.1.5.2.1. Con:
            Virtual cases have enabled courts to address the COVID-19 backlog [380] by reducing the incidence of no-shows [381] and making scheduling and court proceedings more efficient.

        1.3.4.1.6. Pro:
        Jury decisions can occasionally take a long time [382], which is detrimental to the accused and violates their right to a speedy trial [383].

          1.3.4.1.6.1. Pro:
          This is particularly harmful to defendants of lower socioeconomic status, who cannot afford bail [384] and must remain incarcerated until a verdict is reached despite potentially being found innocent.

          1.3.4.1.6.2. Con:
          The average length of jury deliberation in serious cases involving harm to people is just 136.3 minutes [385] and is even shorter for less severe crimes.

      1.3.4.2. Pro:
      Operating a jury necessitates a substantial amount of administrative work by the court (p. 1 [386]).

        1.3.4.2.1. Pro:
        About 32 million [387] Americans are tapped for jury service each year; managing this system requires substantial effort and resources.

          1.3.4.2.1.1. Pro:
          Court administrative costs per juror [388] range from $5 to $20; if all courts operated at maximum efficiency, this would still equate to over $160 million per year.

        1.3.4.2.2. Con:
        Since the 1950s, there has been an increased focus on court administration in the US that has greatly improved court efficiency (p. 21 [389]).

      1.3.4.3. Pro:
      The methods used to select jurors are inefficient [390].

        1.3.4.3.1. Pro:
        Depending on the case and the lawyer, jury selection often takes an inordinately long time (p. 565 [391]).

          1.3.4.3.1.1. Pro:
          Jury selection in high-profile cases can sometimes last months [392].

            1.3.4.3.1.1.1. Con:
            In high-profile cases, finding unbiased jurors [393] can be difficult. The additional time that is spent assembling juries in such cases is a success of the jury system, not a failure.

          1.3.4.3.1.2. Con:
          Virtual jury selection [394], as utilized during the COVID-19 pandemic, has transformed and streamlined the process.

            1.3.4.3.1.2.1. Con:
            Virtual jury selection will result in a less diverse jury pool [395], since virtual jury selection excludes those without a computer and reliable internet access.

        1.3.4.3.2. Pro:
        Since the majority of potential jurors who are called to serve are never utilized in trials [396], courts are not efficiently pre-screening and qualifying [397] potential jurors.

        1.3.4.3.3. Pro:
        The easiest way to streamline the trial process is to eliminate jury selection altogether (pp. 350-351 [398]).

        1.3.4.3.4. Con:
        Rather than abolishing juries altogether, jurors could be selected at random using voter registration.

          1.3.4.3.4.1. Con:
          Millions of eligible Americans are not registered to vote [399] and would be prevented from serving on a jury.

            1.3.4.3.4.1.1. Pro:
            Some people might opt out of registering to vote in order to avoid jury duty; this would be harmful to the democratic process.

          1.3.4.3.4.2. Con:
          American voters have been found to be uniformed and are significantly politically ignorant (p. 1171 [400]). Selecting only voters eliminates people from other walks of life.

          1.3.4.3.4.3. Pro:
          The Sixth Amendment of the Constitution [401], which guarantees US citizens the right to be tried by an impartial jury of their peers, does not stipulate how the jury must be assembled.

        1.3.4.3.5. Con:
        The other long timelines and delays [402] inherent in the legal system dwarf the time spent on jury selection.

          1.3.4.3.5.1. Pro:
          Most juries are selected in a single day [403].

          1.3.4.3.5.2. Pro:
          Growing judicial backlogs can often result in severe delays for trials and even simple legal motions (p. 793 [404]).

          1.3.4.3.5.3. Con:
          There are approximately 154,000 jury trials in the US each year (p. 7 [405]); collectively, they represent a massive waste of time [406] for the members of the juror pool.

        1.3.4.3.6. Con:
        Rather than abolishing juries, jurors could be selected without being vetted by lawyers in order to save time and simplify the process.

          1.3.4.3.6.1. Con:
          Random selection would eliminate voir dire [407], allowing people with extreme and open biases to serve as jurors.

      1.3.4.4. Con:
      Some areas have implemented expedited jury trial processes and improved their jury management systems in order to increase the efficiency [408] of jury trials.

        1.3.4.4.1. Pro:
        Expedited jury trials decrease the number of jurors [409] and the amount of time that jurors must serve.

        1.3.4.4.2. Pro:
        Expedited jury trials can be particularly useful [410] in low-value cases, helping unclog courts.

        1.3.4.4.3. Con:
        Expedited jury trials are not practical for complex or controversial cases, as both parties waive the right to appeal [411].

      1.3.4.5. Con:
      Significantly fewer jury trials than bench trials are heard in the US.

        1.3.4.5.1. Pro:
        Most cases end up with either a plea [412], settlement [413], or dismissal [414] and never go to trial.

        1.3.4.5.2. Con:
        When trials do go to a jury, they take more resources [415] on a per trial basis compared with bench trials.

        1.3.4.5.3. Pro:
        Overall, jury trials are becoming increasingly rare [416] in the court system.

  1.4. Pro:
  Jury trials are more likely than bench trials to result in biased outcomes.

    1.4.1. Pro:
    Jury members can be intimidated or influenced [417] in order to sway their decision.

      1.4.1.1. Pro:
      The three-judge, no jury Special Criminal Court [418] of Ireland was established in the 1970s after jury members were intimidated into giving notorious criminals leniency (p. 94 [419]).

        1.4.1.1.1. Con:
        Ireland had a difficult time containing criminal activity during the Troubles [420]; jury intimidation is no longer as simple or straightforward.

        1.4.1.1.2. Con:
        There have been calls to abolish [421] the SCC, as it was established as a temporary emergency measure and is no longer necessary.

        1.4.1.1.3. Pro:
