        The SCC has continued to prove its resilience, helping disband hit squads in 2021 by rapidly imprisoning [422] those involved.

      1.4.1.2. Pro:
      Relatives of defendants [423] occasionally attempt to sway jurors by making impassioned pleas for leniency.

        1.4.1.2.1. Con:
        If all the proper protocols [424] are followed during cases with anonymous juries, relatives of defendants should not have access to the identities of jury members.

        1.4.1.2.2. Pro:
        Since the vast majority of cases do not meet the qualifications for an anonymous jury, jurors are generally easy to identify (p. 5 [425]).

      1.4.1.3. Con:
      There is no need to abolish jury trials, since the justice system can instead forgo jury trials in cases where there is a high probability of jury tampering [426].

        1.4.1.3.1. Con:
        Given the wide range of predisposing conditions and behaviors that qualify as jury tampering, it could be exceedingly difficult to identify in advance which cases might be likely targets (p. 772 [427]).

        1.4.1.3.2. Pro:
        In cases involving celebrities or politicians, there is increased likelihood of jury tampering due to the intense scrutiny of juror backgrounds [428].

      1.4.1.4. Con:
      Judges are subject to intimidation and manipulation (p. 30 [429]).

        1.4.1.4.1. Con:
        Judges have more protections [430] than ordinary members of the jury, so intimidating or manipulating judges is more difficult.

        1.4.1.4.2. Pro:
        There were over 4,500 registered threats [431] made against US federal judges in 2021, and the annual number has continued to rise.

      1.4.1.5. Con:
      Any attempt to sway or influence a member of the jury would be considered jury tampering [432], which would result in the case being thrown out.

        1.4.1.5.1. Con:
        In some cases, jury tampering is intended [433] to result in a mistrial.

        1.4.1.5.2. Pro:
        Simply providing a juror with outside information about the case they are trying constitutes jury tampering [434].

    1.4.2. Pro:
    Diminishing the potential for biased outcomes is already achieved by the appeals system [435], without the need for a jury.

      1.4.2.1. Pro:
      A robust appeals system acts as a powerful incentive to eliminate bias by holding judges accountable for every verdict (p. 15 [436]). Having their verdict overturned upon review is embarrassing for judges and can tarnish their reputation (p. 21 [437]).

        1.4.2.1.1. Con:
        Appeals do not effectively address bias, as they are generally only granted to address errors in the interpretation or application [438] of a law.

          1.4.2.1.1.1. Con:
          There is precedent for considering a high probability of judicial bias [439] as sufficient cause for granting an appeal; judicial bias need not be definitively proven.

        1.4.2.1.2. Pro:
        Judges have a professional reputation to maintain and will be criticized if they do not consider the facts of a case sufficiently (p. 1245 [440]).

      1.4.2.2. Con:
      Appeals should not be required to avoid bias; as they can be both expensive and time consuming [441], they may not be an option for all of the accused.

        1.4.2.2.1. Pro:
        In complex cases, an appeal can cost the defendant up to $30,000 [442].

        1.4.2.2.2. Con:
        In Canada, those who cannot afford to pay the court filing fees have the option [443] to apply for an order that no fees are payable.

          1.4.2.2.2.1. Con:
          Defendants who are given a fee waiver are still accountable for transcript costs [444] and, if the appeal is unsuccessful, the other party's costs. This could amount to thousands of dollars.

      1.4.2.3. Con:
      Repeated appeals are incredibly inefficient, as they require several times the amount of resources that a single jury case consumes.

        1.4.2.3.1. Con:
        In as many as 26.3% of capital appeals, the judgment of the previous court is overturned (p. 23 [445]). This indicates that the inefficiency of appeals is a necessary cost of ensuring that justice is done.

        1.4.2.3.2. Pro:
        More than 40% of tried jury cases are appealed, straining an already fragile system (p. 660 [446]).

      1.4.2.4. Pro:
      A significant number of judges have reviewed a case and agreed on the correct verdict by the time a defendant runs out of appeals [447].

        1.4.2.4.1. Con:
        Judges being in agreement with one another does not equate to them being correct.

        1.4.2.4.2. Pro:
        Courts of appeals apply different degrees of scrutiny to cases based on the issue under consideration; thus, more serious questions of bias are held to higher standards of review (pp. 1-2 [448]).

    1.4.3. Con:
    Regardless of how judges are chosen, their verdicts are often influenced by politics (pp. 377-378 [449]).

      1.4.3.1. Con:
      There is little evidence to suggest that the verdicts of juries aren't similarly influenced by politics.

        1.4.3.1.1. Con:
        The requirement for unanimity in jury decisions helps to depoliticize jury verdicts by strongly incentivizing compromise (p. 43 [450]).

        1.4.3.1.2. Pro:
        Juries are subject to the effects of group polarization, whereby deliberation often reinforces the preexisting political beliefs of individual jury members (pp. 3-4 [451]).

      1.4.3.2. Pro:
      Many judges have their own political beliefs and legislate from the bench [452] rather than enforce the law. Jury decisions are not subject to the politics of a single person.

        1.4.3.2.1. Pro:
        Both liberal and conservative justices are guilty of legislating from the bench [453], suggesting consistent political bias from the judiciary.

        1.4.3.2.2. Pro:
        Judges who are appointed by legislatures, such as those in Canada [454], are likely to reflect the political views of the legislature in their verdicts.

      1.4.3.3. Pro:
      Judges who are selected through political means are likely to retain their partisanship.

        1.4.3.3.1. Con:
        The majority of cases that judges preside over involve routine criminal and civil matters [455] rather than politically charged issues.

        1.4.3.3.2. Pro:
        Judges tend to serve long terms. This means they may effectively advance a political agenda [456] no longer supported by the public.

          1.4.3.3.2.1. Pro:
          Federal and state judges who are appointed [457] are likely to reflect the politics of those who appoint [458] them.

            1.4.3.3.2.1.1. Pro:
            Supreme Court justices are often selected precisely because of their political stance on important topics; for example, President Trump insisted on appointing judges who would overturn Roe v. Wade [459].

          1.4.3.3.2.2. Con:
          Judges have limited opportunity to pursue political agendas in comparison with legislative or executive [460] politicians.

            1.4.3.3.2.2.1. Con:
            There is empirical evidence that the decisions of judges are consistently influenced by their politics and other biases (p. 1 [461]). Even if the effects of such biases on an individual case are small, over many years and cases, this can have tangible effects and constitute an agenda.

            1.4.3.3.2.2.2. Pro:
            Judges are only able to make decisions on the cases which they are assigned to and thus cannot proactively affect policy (p. 853 [462]).

            1.4.3.3.2.2.3. Con:
            The US judiciary has become highly politicized, with judges increasingly appointed based on ideology rather than qualifications (p. 577 [463]).

          1.4.3.3.2.3. Pro:
          Judges appointed for life, such as in the US Supreme Court, have little incentive [464] to maintain their intellectual rigor or objectivity once they have the position.

            1.4.3.3.2.3.1. Con:
            Judges that are appointed for life [465] stand to gain little by seeking the favor of politicians or political parties.

        1.4.3.3.3. Pro:
        Thirty-nine US states elect judges [466], making their verdicts particularly subject to partisan and ideological biases.

          1.4.3.3.3.1. Pro:
          Elected judges also accept bribes more often than appointed judges relative to the amount of cases they handle (p. 1924 [467]).

          1.4.3.3.3.2. Pro:
          Judges may reach certain verdicts in an attempt to be re-elected rather than to serve justice.

            1.4.3.3.3.2.1. Pro:
            The US Supreme Court has ruled that excessive financial contributions to judges' election campaigns are a threat to fair trials [468].

            1.4.3.3.3.2.2. Pro:
            Judges that rely on voters for re-election [469] are more likely to cater decisions towards the prevailing political beliefs of their constituents, regardless of their actual position (p. 169 [470]).

              1.4.3.3.3.2.2.1. Pro:
              In states where judges are elected, the likelihood of judges affirming death sentences is directly related to the local approval rate for capital punishment (p. 2 [471]).

                1.4.3.3.3.2.2.1.1. Pro:
                Between 2000 and 2015, appointed judges in the US reversed death sentences at a much higher rate than elected judges: more than double the rate of judges facing competitive elections (p. 2 [472]).

              1.4.3.3.3.2.2.2. Pro:
              Elected judges have a strong incentive to appear to be tough on crime in order to be re-elected (p. 5 [473]), handing down harsher sentences and more liberally applying the death penalty in the year before an election (p. 8 [474]).

              1.4.3.3.3.2.2.3. Con:
              Verdicts that reflect the public view on how to handle crime are a positive thing, as they reinforce community norms and values [475].

                1.4.3.3.3.2.2.3.1. Con:
                Community norms and values can be reinforced by the community members themselves; outsider validation is not necessary.

                1.4.3.3.3.2.2.3.2. Pro:
                Reinforcing community norms and values helps build stronger communities, which reduces crime in the long run (p. 3 [476]).

                1.4.3.3.3.2.2.3.3. Con:
                Public opinion is not a valid basis for judicial decisions; it is not subject to codified law and is highly subject to framing [477] by politicians and media sources.

            1.4.3.3.3.2.3. Con:
            Judges are almost universally re-elected [478], regardless of their performance, suggesting that they do not need to pander to public opinion.

              1.4.3.3.3.2.3.1. Pro:
              Meaningfully measuring judicial performance is very difficult (p. 250 [479]).

              1.4.3.3.3.2.3.2. Con:
              The re-election of judges regardless of their performance may plausibly be a result of a poor public understanding of the justice system (p. 10 [480]).

              1.4.3.3.3.2.3.3. Con:
              In some states, performance evaluations [481] of judges are provided to voters prior to retention elections.

                1.4.3.3.3.2.3.3.1. Con:
                Only seven of the thirty-nine states [482] that elect judges use performance reviews to support voter choice.

          1.4.3.3.3.3. Con:
          Jurors have their own political beliefs and affiliations as well. Their verdicts are no less likely to be influenced by political factors.

        1.4.3.3.4. Con:
        Judges can and have been impeached [483] for acting in a partisan or otherwise inappropriate manner.

    1.4.4. Con:
    Juries are more likely to diminish bias because they are composed of 6-12 individuals who must agree on a unanimous decision [484].

      1.4.4.1. Pro:
      A jury represents a substantially larger sample size than a single judge.

        1.4.4.1.1. Pro:
        Larger sample sizes, and thus larger juries, would further reduce the margin of error [485].

          1.4.4.1.1.1. Con:
          Larger jury sizes suffer from increasing inefficiency and deliberation time; research indicates that the optimal jury size is nine to fifteen people (p. 5 [486]).

        1.4.4.1.2. Con:
        The small size of juries [487] makes them particularly subject to groupthink; the individual positions of the jurors can shift in favor [488] of the majority consensus, minimizing dissent.

          1.4.4.1.2.1. Con:
          Dissent is a common occurrence among juries. Trials that end up with a hung jury have an average of three dissenting jurors [489].

          1.4.4.1.2.2. Pro:
          In Sweden, the verdicts of jury members are often influenced by the political ideology [490] of their fellow jurors.

      1.4.4.2. Con:
      The jury selection process does not effectively reduce bias.

        1.4.4.2.1. Con:
        There are techniques that can effectively reduce the impact of jury bias during jury selection (pp. 684-685 [491]).

          1.4.4.2.1.1. Con:
          It is possible for both the prosecution and defense to play off of each others' biases during jury selection, both consciously and subconsciously.

          1.4.4.2.1.2. Pro:
          Mixing biases [492], such as including those who believe in the judicial system and those who do not, can help mitigate the effects of juror bias.

            1.4.4.2.1.2.1. Con:
            Prior negative experiences [493] with the judicial system or a belief that the system never makes mistakes are sufficient reasons for dismissing a juror (p. 166 [494]).

          1.4.4.2.1.3. Con:
          Jury members can lie about their biases during voir dire interviews in order to hide their true intentions (p. 668 [495]).

        1.4.4.2.2. Pro:
        When a case is widely publicized, it can be difficult to select [496] jurors whose opinions have not been biased by the media's presentation of the case.

          1.4.4.2.2.1. Pro:
          Many jurors misunderstand the laws regarding contempt of court [497] and research their case on the internet.

          1.4.4.2.2.2. Pro:
          The standards for juror impartiality [498] had to be lowered in the Boston Marathon bombing case, as only a few of the potential jurors were unbiased and open to the possibility of the death penalty.

          1.4.4.2.2.3. Con:
          The vast majority of trials are for traffic-related offenses or minor crimes [499] and receive no public attention; media bias is only a factor in a small percentage of trials.

            1.4.4.2.2.3.1. Con:
            Media bias need not be specific to the case at hand. Potential jurors can also be exposed to media bias related to the issues, laws, or principles of a case (p. 2 [500]).

            1.4.4.2.2.3.2. Pro:
            In important public cases with increased media scrutiny, cases can be retried [501] if evidence of jury bias is discovered.

              1.4.4.2.2.3.2.1. Con:
              During voir dire, additional attention should be devoted to weeding out juror bias in order to avoid retrials of high-profile cases (p. 676 [502]).

              1.4.4.2.2.3.2.2. Con:
              In cases with increased media scrutiny, attorneys can try to use the media to their advantage [503] in order to argue for a mistrial if the outcome is not in their client's favor.

              1.4.4.2.2.3.2.3. Pro:
              Jurors themselves create additional risk when they speak to the media in high-profile cases, revealing unperceived biases [504] that would have otherwise remained hidden.

              1.4.4.2.2.3.2.4. Pro:
              Jurors are instructed to ignore any bias they may have formed due to exposure to the media (p. 3 [505]).

                1.4.4.2.2.3.2.4.1. Con:
                Instructing a jury to ignore bias from the media is less effective than preventing them from being exposed to media bias in the first place.

            1.4.4.2.2.3.3. Con:
            There is no clear definition of what constitutes a high-profile case. Some cases may receive local media coverage [506] but fail to make national headlines.

          1.4.4.2.2.4. Con:
          Uninformed juries are less likely to have been influenced by media coverage and opinions relevant to a case.

            1.4.4.2.2.4.1. Pro:
            -> See 1.2.3.3.3.1.

            1.4.4.2.2.4.2. Con:
            Uninformed juries produce incorrect outcomes [507], jeopardizing the entire judicial system.

        1.4.4.2.3. Pro:
        Jury members are a reflection of a biased [508] and prejudicial [509] society.

          1.4.4.2.3.1. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.3.2.

          1.4.4.2.3.2. Con:
          Jury members with biases and prejudices can still deliver a fair and just verdict [510].

          1.4.4.2.3.3. Con:
          As time goes on, there is less [511] societal prejudice. The views of jury members will adjust accordingly.

          1.4.4.2.3.4. Pro:
          Jurors carry the biases that they have developed [512] throughout their lives into the courtroom.

            1.4.4.2.3.4.1. Pro:
            The selection of juries from within the local community can reinforce local and regional biases.

        1.4.4.2.4. Con:
        -> See 1.1.6.1.2.

      1.4.4.3. Con:
      Unanimity does not necessarily counter bias, since more powerful or outspoken jurors can sway the rest of the jury in accordance with their prejudices.

        1.4.4.3.1. Pro:
        Requiring unanimity can incentivize jurors to make rash decisions in order to get back to their regular lives (p. 218 [513]).

          1.4.4.3.1.1. Pro:
          Jurors are subject to substantial stress while serving due to concerns about work, childcare, disturbing subject matter, and public opinion of the case (pp. 2-3 [514]).

        1.4.4.3.2. Pro:
        A single biased juror can unduly influence the rest of the jury, whereas a judge with a biased history [515] can be taken off a case entirely.

          1.4.4.3.2.1. Pro:
          Jurors with strong opinions can contribute to confirmation bias [516]; one outspoken juror can confirm everyone's initial biases and affect the overall verdict.

          1.4.4.3.2.2. Con:
          In Brazil, judges can decide for themselves if they are unfit to serve on a case (pp. 780-781 [517]), which has led to high-profile cases [518] of confirmed judicial bias.

        1.4.4.3.3. Con:
        A single juror who engages in critical analysis can convince a prejudiced jury to objectively analyze the facts, as in the classic movie "12 Angry Men [519]."

          1.4.4.3.3.1. Pro:
          Requiring unanimity empowers the opinion of each individual juror, which has been demonstrated to reduce the conviction ratio [520].

          1.4.4.3.3.2. Con:
          Holdout jurors often yield to pressure from other jurors rather than delaying the verdict to address their objections (p. 587 [521]).

          1.4.4.3.3.3. Con:
          A fictional movie is not necessarily reflective of reality.

      1.4.4.4. Pro:
      The requirement of unanimity forces the jurors to consider multiple perspectives to reach a consensus. No comparable incentive acts on judges.

        1.4.4.4.1. Con:
        Some US states do not require unanimous verdicts [522] in criminal cases, while nearly one-third of the states require only a majority verdict in civil cases.

        1.4.4.4.2. Con:
        Since judges must provide a written judgment [523], they have greater incentive to consider the facts, as jurors are not required to justify their reasoning.

          1.4.4.4.2.1. Con:
          Instead of abolishing juries, jurors could also be required to provide a written explanation of their reasoning.

        1.4.4.4.3. Pro:
        A single person cannot realistically consider the facts of a case from as many perspectives as a larger group.

          1.4.4.4.3.1. Con:
          Panels [524] composed of three judges presiding together over a case can curb the risk of undue influence over any single judge.

            1.4.4.4.3.1.1. Con:
            The judicial systems of many countries are chronically underfunded and understaffed [525]. Requiring three judges to preside over each case would mean that fewer cases could be presented to the bench, delaying justice.

            1.4.4.4.3.1.2. Pro:
            As a three-judge ruling is a synthesis of different opinions, these judgments are less prone to error and more legitimate than the decisions of a single judge (p. 3 [526]).

          1.4.4.4.3.2. Pro:
          Considering the facts of a case from the diverse perspectives of other jurors makes juries more likely to empathize with the defendant, more familiar with the nuances of the case, and more able to generate solid arguments for their opinions; these characteristics contribute to just verdicts [527].

    1.4.5. Pro:
    Juries are more likely than judges to be swayed in their decision-making by emotional or irrational factors (pp. 1008-1009 [528]).

      1.4.5.1. Pro:
      Jury decisions are often based on popular belief, misinformed, and rooted in emotions [529] that can do more harm than good (pp. 32-33 [530]); public opinion should not be reflected in the rulings of the justice system (p. 131 [531]).

        1.4.5.1.1. Pro:
        Strong negative emotions, in particular anger and disgust, are associated with a higher conviction rate by juries (p. 357 [532]).

          1.4.5.1.1.1. Con:
          While the effects are somewhat less pronounced, judicial verdicts display a pattern of affective influence comparable to jury verdicts (pp. 900, 911 [533]).

          1.4.5.1.1.2. Pro:
          Jury decisions are often based on moral values; therefore, juries are an ineffective method for adjudicating matters of law, as moral values often do not track with legality (pp. 1290-1291 [534]).

        1.4.5.1.2. Con:
        The ability of juries to take context and emotion into account is beneficial to the judicial system.

          1.4.5.1.2.1. Con:
          Criminal defendants are often coached [535] by their legal counsel to display the specific emotions and responses that are likely to sway the jury's opinion.

          1.4.5.1.2.2. Pro:
          The emotion expressed by a witness or defendant may reflect the truthfulness of their testimony, and jurors are capable of using this to help determine the truth of such expressions.

            1.4.5.1.2.2.1. Con:
            Most people are not adept at assessing the truth of emotions expressed by others (p. 9 [536]).

            1.4.5.1.2.2.2. Pro:
            Analysis of involuntary microexpressions can reveal inconsistencies that experts can use to evaluate the veracity of a statement (p. 10 [537]).

            1.4.5.1.2.2.3. Pro:
            Several US states have introduced social science frameworks to educate jurors and have them take into consideration the multitude of factors which affect an eyewitness' memory (p. 133 [538]).

            1.4.5.1.2.2.4. Con:
            People with psychopathy can often control their emotions in order to manipulate [539] others. Such a defendant could display certain emotions to gain jurors' sympathies.

            1.4.5.1.2.2.5. Con:
            Judges are better than juries at assessing the truthfulness of testimony; over time, they have developed clearly delineated techniques and approaches [540] for doing so.

          1.4.5.1.2.3. Con:
          Jurors should rely on reason and critical thinking rather than their emotions to reach a sensible verdict (pp. 16-17 [541]); emotional jurors hinder this process.

            1.4.5.1.2.3.1. Pro:
            When jurors make decisions based on emotions, they are more likely to be influenced by ingrained prejudices [542], such as racism.

            1.4.5.1.2.3.2. Con:
            Emotional factors [543] are necessary to help jurors determine the credibility of witnesses in subtle ways that pure reasoning cannot.

          1.4.5.1.2.4. Pro:
          Emotions are frequently considered valid evidence that can be used in court.

            1.4.5.1.2.4.1. Con:
            It is difficult to shield jurors from emotionally charged evidence, which often works against the purported goal of a fair trial (pp. 23-24 [544]).

            1.4.5.1.2.4.2. Pro:
            A charge of second-degree murder [545] can be reduced to voluntary manslaughter if the facts demonstrate that the defendant was adequately provoked. Such a consideration requires jurors to consider the emotions of the defendant as facts in the case.

            1.4.5.1.2.4.3. Pro:
            In the case of larceny [546], jurors have to consider a defendant's beliefs and emotions to determine whether the accused believed in self-ownership of the property or intended to deprive someone else of their property.

        1.4.5.1.3. Con:
        When properly evaluated, public opinion tends to support positions that are similar to established case precedent (p. 395 [547]).

        1.4.5.1.4. Pro:
        Jurors' views are highly subject to outside influence; exposing jurors to misinformation during deliberations can alter their memories of the case and skew their verdicts (p. 21 [548]).

      1.4.5.2. Con:
      Jurors can be taught to be aware of their implicit biases, enabling them to return verdicts that are based on the facts of the case (pp. 829-830 [549]).

        1.4.5.2.1. Con:
        The effectiveness of anti-bias training is questionable (p. 59 [550]).

        1.4.5.2.2. Pro:
        Some courts have allowed attorneys to call implicit bias experts as witnesses when it is especially relevant to the case (p. 87 [551]).

        1.4.5.2.3. Pro:
        In some cases, jury instructions have been expanded to include an element that educates jurors on implicit bias and how to eliminate it (pp. 89-90 [552]).

          1.4.5.2.3.1. Con:
          In the majority of cases, jurors receive virtually no training [553] regarding the intricacies of the law or critical thinking skills.

        1.4.5.2.4. Pro:
        New Jersey has instituted implicit bias training [554] for jurors in order to reduce the impact of bias on case outcomes.

        1.4.5.2.5. Con:
        Judges often deny requests [555] for implicit bias training, limiting its potential as a strategy to reduce bias.

        1.4.5.2.6. Con:
        There are very limited opportunities to address a jury's implicit bias (p. 190 [556]). Waiting for societal and personal attitudes to change is too risky when a defendant's life may be at stake.

      1.4.5.3. Con:
      The severity of judges' sentencing decisions is correlated with the timing of their meals and breaks (p. 6889 [557]).

        1.4.5.3.1. Con:
        There are a number of possible confounding variables [558] that could explain this correlation, such as procedural influence on the types of cases scheduled at different times of the day, or the existence of a real or implied quota of positive rulings that a judge is expected to deliver.

        1.4.5.3.2. Pro:
        Both judges and juries are inherently subject to human limitations, such as fluctuations in mood and attention [559]. Such effects are offset by the larger group size of juries.

      1.4.5.4. Pro:
      Judges are specifically trained to be impartial and consider only the facts [560].

        1.4.5.4.1. Con:
        Unlike juries, judges may be too overqualified and rigid to provide a reasonable layperson's perspective [561].

        1.4.5.4.2. Pro:
        Appeals to the jury often rely on prejudice, fear, or other emotional arguments that would not persuade a judge (p. 2 [562]).

          1.4.5.4.2.1. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.2.4.1.3.1.

          1.4.5.4.2.2. Con:
          Many judges are subject to influence from emotional appeals (p. 1485 [563]).

          1.4.5.4.2.3. Pro:
          Judges are legally required to recuse themselves [564] from cases where they have an actual or apprehended bias for or against either party.

        1.4.5.4.3. Pro:
        Judges are frequently selected based on their ability to rule impartially and often have such a reputation, even with the opposing side [565].

        1.4.5.4.4. Con:
        Although judges are trained to be impartial, this does not mean they always act so.

          1.4.5.4.4.1. Con:
          Judges can undergo additional training to focus specifically on remaining impartial during cases.

            1.4.5.4.4.1.1. Pro:
            Frequent training sessions are more effective at reducing the implicit biases held by judges in comparison with one-off events (p. 1 [566]).

            1.4.5.4.4.1.2. Pro:
            Judicial focus groups have recommended specific training methods that could be employed by courts to reduce implicit bias in judges and court officials (pp. 1, 6-7 [567]).

            1.4.5.4.4.1.3. Con:
            Juries are equally capable of undergoing training to focus on remaining impartial.

              1.4.5.4.4.1.3.1. Con:
              A number of resources are required to train multi-member juries for just one case. Increasing the amount of time and resources required for training will slow down or overburden the process.

            1.4.5.4.4.1.4. Con:
            Bias training for judges may be ineffective; research has demonstrated that judges are often unaware of their implicit biases and overconfident in their ability to rule impartially [568].

          1.4.5.4.4.2. Pro:
          Since judges [569] are expected to be unbiased and impartial, their rulings are not often questioned [570], even when incorrect.

            1.4.5.4.4.2.1. Con:
            In high-profile cases [571], judges' rulings are often questioned.

          1.4.5.4.4.3. Pro:
          Judges can make wrong decisions [572] based on a disregard for the law or a lack of empathy for the defendant; these factors are mitigated by juries.

            1.4.5.4.4.3.1. Pro:
            In the infamous "affluenza case [573]," a judge accepted a ridiculous malady made up by the defendant's legal team, with no regard for precedent.

    1.4.6. Pro:
    Judges have the training and expertise [574] to understand complex law better than juries.

      1.4.6.1. Con:
      In the United States, there are no requirements [575] as to who can become a federal judge, as they are appointed by the president.

        1.4.6.1.1. Con:
        Per Article 2, Section 2 of the US Constitution [576], the Senate must confirm the president's federal judiciary nominees.

          1.4.6.1.1.1. Pro:
          While anyone can, in theory, become a federal judge, the process for nominating, rating, evaluating, and voting on nominees typically only allows highly qualified candidates to be appointed (pp. 1-2 [577]).

        1.4.6.1.2. Pro:
        The majority of historical US Supreme Court justices have not had any judicial experience [578].

          1.4.6.1.2.1. Con:
          This has shifted in recent times; twelve of the fourteen justices appointed to the Supreme Court since 1975 have had prior judicial experience [579].

      1.4.6.2. Pro:
      Judges have a comprehensive understanding [580] of complex legal issues; a layperson cannot be expected to match their level of knowledge.

        1.4.6.2.1. Pro:
        The majority of judges [581] have acquired bachelor's and law degrees and have previously worked as attorneys; it is not feasible to train all potential jurors up to this same standard.

          1.4.6.2.1.1. Pro:
          In most US states, becoming a judge takes many years [582]: at least seven years of school and several additional years of experience as an attorney.

        1.4.6.2.2. Con:
        Juror training courses could be established to help people better comprehend and adjust to their role on a jury (p. 303 [583]).

          1.4.6.2.2.1. Con:
          Jury duty is already enough of a pain; adding additional requirements is likely to make people even less interested in accepting their responsibility as a juror.

          1.4.6.2.2.2. Pro:
          Such training could stipulate that any potential juror unable to achieve a reasonable level of competence would be excused, as they would be unfit to serve (pp. 303, 309, 312-313 [584]).

        1.4.6.2.3. Pro:
        Judges are cognizant of legal precedent [585] and more aware of the nuances of the law. This makes them more discerning than juries.

          1.4.6.2.3.1. Pro:
          Laws must often be interpreted; legal training makes one better qualified to do this.

            1.4.6.2.3.1.1. Pro:
            Judges are taught to use five overlapping methods [586] of interpreting laws.

              1.4.6.2.3.1.1.1. Con:
              Because there are two predominant theories of judicial interpretation [587], different judges considering the same law can have very distinct interpretations.

            1.4.6.2.3.1.2. Con:
            Interpretation of the law is the role [588] of judges; jurors need not be able to do this as judges can simplify their interpretation of the law for jurors.

          1.4.6.2.3.2. Con:
          Legal precedent is generally simplified into clear rules [589] that can be taught to the jury through instructions.

        1.4.6.2.4. Con:
        Judges are permitted to explain legal terms [590] to jurors when they are unsure about the meaning or impact of a given law.

          1.4.6.2.4.1. Pro:
          When judges provide juries with clear and explicit instructions, they make it easier for jurors to understand the case and deliberate more thoughtfully (p. 2 [591]).

          1.4.6.2.4.2. Con:
          Jurors often misunderstand [592] the legal instructions given by judges.

            1.4.6.2.4.2.1. Con:
            Improved training for judges would enable them to provide jurors with more understandable instructions.

          1.4.6.2.4.3. Con:
          Judges are often prohibited from giving juries instructions that go beyond an explanation of the relevant legal terminology [593].

      1.4.6.3. Pro:
      Judges have a better understanding of the standard of proof beyond a reasonable doubt [594].

        1.4.6.3.1. Con:
        There is no judicial consensus [595] as to the meaning of proof beyond a reasonable doubt.

        1.4.6.3.2. Pro:
        In Australia, the meaning of proof beyond a reasonable doubt is often unclear [596] to jurors, and judges are not permitted to provide them any clarification in order to avoid influencing their interpretations.

      1.4.6.4. Con:
      Some areas use non-lawyer judges [597], who may not understand all the complexities of the law.

        1.4.6.4.1. Pro:
        In eight US states, non-lawyer judges [598] can incarcerate people, and a defendant has no right to be retried by a lawyer-judge.

        1.4.6.4.2. Pro:
        As judges are not required to have legal experience, arguing for the abolition of juries based on their inexperience is unreasonable.

        1.4.6.4.3. Con:
        In 28 US states [599], judges must have previously been lawyers.

        1.4.6.4.4. Con:
        Non-lawyer judges may only impose jail sentences of up to six months, and in 14 states the convicted can appeal their sentences [600].

      1.4.6.5. Pro:
      Cases involving financial crimes or complex forensic evidence are often difficult for juries to fully understand; therefore, juries are less qualified to make judgments than judges or experts in the subject matter (pp. 1-2 [601]).

        1.4.6.5.1. Pro:
        The highly specialized knowledge of expert witnesses can be considered a form of evidence that is not presented before the court, and their expertise may itself constitute bias (p. 496 [602]).

        1.4.6.5.2. Con:
        Linear Sequential Masking [603] and other techniques to reduce bias among forensic experts allow juries to hear evidence from expert witnesses that has not been tainted by knowledge of yet-to-be-presented or excluded pieces of evidence.

        1.4.6.5.3. Con:
        Expert juries [604] with specific knowledge can be used for trials that involve complex issues.

        1.4.6.5.4. Pro:
        Defendants may unfairly benefit [605] from jury trials in cases involving complex white-collar crimes, as prosecutors are forced to synthesize difficult information in a way that laypeople will understand.

        1.4.6.5.5. Pro:
        The increased use of technology [606] as evidence in criminal cases has created an additional burden on juries; the need to understand more complex concepts can extend the length of a trial and further disrupt jurors' lives.

      1.4.6.6. Con:
      Judges can become jaded and inattentive [607] after having tried thousands of cases in their career.

        1.4.6.6.1. Con:
        Judges are dedicated professionals who have chosen a career presiding over legal cases (p. 6 [608]); thus, they are less likely to be inattentive than jurors, who may be uninterested in a case.

          1.4.6.6.1.1. Con:
          Having chosen their career does not equate to judges being attentive; even people who are happy with their career choice can become bored or disinterested [609] from time to time.

        1.4.6.6.2. Pro:
        Some judges handle [610] more than a thousand cases each year, so they are likely to experience burnout and compassion fatigue [611].

          1.4.6.6.2.1. Con:
          Like all professionals, judges are expected to maintain a sustainable workload and manage their time wisely (p. 6 [612]).

        1.4.6.6.3. Pro:
        Jurors serve on a single case and are therefore more likely to remain attentive [613].

        1.4.6.6.4. Con:
        Since judges try many cases, they can be held accountable for patterns of incompetence or bias [614]. There is no comparable mechanism for jurors, who only try a single case.

          1.4.6.6.4.1. Con:
          The justice system should require a high level of competence, given that defendants' lives and livelihoods are at stake. It is immoral to wait for a judge to establish a pattern of incompetence [615] prior to censuring them.

    1.4.7. Con:
    Many people believe that juries [616] are more likely than judges to deliver a fair verdict.

      1.4.7.1. Con:
      The number of people who believe something is not evidence of its veracity.

        1.4.7.1.1. Pro:
        Despite public perception that judges are more likely to convict, federal judges are significantly more likely than juries to acquit a defendant across all crime categories in the US (p. 224 [617]).

        1.4.7.1.2. Con:
        Trusting one's gut instinct [618] is usually a basic barometer of when something could be off; if many people distrust a person or system, it should generally be considered untrustworthy—regardless of veracity, which is often impossible to prove in reality.

          1.4.7.1.2.1. Con:
          The parent claim commits the fallacies of appeal to intuition [619] and appeal to common belief [620].

      1.4.7.2. Pro:
      Public trust [621] in the judicial system is higher in countries that make use of juries.

        1.4.7.2.1. Con:
        In Taiwan, 84% of those surveyed do not trust judges [622].

          1.4.7.2.1.1. Pro:
          The Taiwanese government decided to integrate citizens into the legal process to reduce dissatisfaction with judges (p. 203 [623]).

          1.4.7.2.1.2. Con:
          Taiwan is an outlier and a unique case; citizens distrust the impartiality of the judiciary [624] due to the influence of mainland China, not the judges themselves.

        1.4.7.2.2. Pro:
        Juries have been demonstrated to be thoughtful, conservative, and trustworthy [625], gaining public confidence in the process.

      1.4.7.3. Con:
      Ordinary citizens might not be able to identify with or trust judges because the perspective of the average person is closer to that of a juror [626] than a judge.

        1.4.7.3.1. Pro:
        Defendants typically prefer jury trials because judges are typically viewed as case hardened and emotionless [627] compared with juries.

        1.4.7.3.2. Pro:
        Judges are expected to cultivate an unusual degree of detachment in order to rule impartially (pp. 607, 610 [628]).

        1.4.7.3.3. Con:
        According to public perception, judges are known for deciding cases based on the law rather than political concerns (p. 57 [629]).

      1.4.7.4. Pro:
      Leaving the fate of the accused in the hands of a single person is hazardous.

        1.4.7.4.1. Pro:
        Judges remain subject to human error despite their training and best efforts (p. 351 [630]).

        1.4.7.4.2. Pro:
        The larger group offered by a jury reduces the margin of error (p. 31 [631]).

        1.4.7.4.3. Con:
        Appellate courts in the US make use of three-judge panels [632] to rule on challenges to a trial court's decision.

          1.4.7.4.3.1. Con:
          It is uncommon that a panel of judges is persuaded to reconsider a previous judge's decision by reading a written brief; less than one-quarter [633] of appeals proceed to oral arguments.

Sources:
[1] https://via.library.depaul.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?referer=&httpsredir=1&article=2860&context=law-review
"In ordinary criminal trials, it is more often the state rather than the federal government who is the prosecutor. Following passage of the fourteenth amendment in 1868, the question arose as to whether federal standards would be imposed on state juries. In Maxwell v. Dow, a divided Court followed the precedent of the Slaughterhouse Cases and sharply distinguished between federal and state jury rights: "There is no intimation here that among the privileges or immunities of a citizen of the United States [is] the right of trial by jury in a state court for a state offense .... - The Court reasoned that the right to jury trial was not derived from citizenship, but existed in favor of all individuals as against the federal government's power. If a state chose, it could dispense with jury trial, the Court having never affirmed jury trial to be required by due process of law."
[2] https://www.miamicriminaldefenselaw.com/federal-jurisdiction-versus-state-jurisdiction.html
"State court criminal cases are usually investigated by local or statewide police agencies and are prosecuted by the state attorney’s office (known in some areas as the district attorney.) The cases are heard by the state court judge who may be an elected position or appointed by the governor.

The vast majority of criminal cases are prosecuted in state court because most cases do not have a basis for federal jurisdiction. State court includes the most common violent street crimes as for example rape, murder, sexual assault, and armed robberies. State court also includes property offenses such as thefts, car thefts, burglaries, and shop lifting. Economic and white collar crimes such as state tax fraud, check kiting, consumer fraud can be prosecuted in state court."
[3] https://www.upcounsel.com/jury-trial-waiver
"Almost everyone is familiar with the seventh amendment's guarantee of a trial by jury. In some cases, a criminal defendant may opt for a bench trial instead.

A jury trial waiver provides that a party can waive its seventh amendment right. The court system will still handle all disputes, including discovery and appeals, but a judge makes his ruling unanimously.

Bench trials are undoubtedly quicker and cheaper than jury trials. In a jury trial, a jury must be chosen, instructed, and given time to deliberate. If a trial goes on for a long period of time, the jury must also be fed and housed.

In lieu of a jury trial, some choose to have their disputes settled by the court, on the condition that a judge makes the final decision."
[4] https://www.upcounsel.com/jury-trial-waiver
"Previously, it was understood that a criminal defendant had a right to a jury trial, unless there were legal specifications that gave the accused the option of choosing a bench trial. However, in 1930 the U.S. Supreme Court decided a case that indirectly paved the way for bench trials.

In this case, the Court ruled it was acceptable for the defendant to agree that just 11 jurors could decide the case since the 12th juror became ill. The judges continued by saying that once a 12-person jury is dispensed with, you may as well have no jury.

This justification by the court concerned public policy because the state has an interest in preserving and protecting the lives and liberties of its citizens. This interest is furthered by placing the responsibility of finding someone guilty on 12 people instead of only one person.

Therefore, the court noted that the prosecutor and the court must agree before waiving a jury trial."
[5] https://judicature.duke.edu/articles/going-going-but-not-quite-gone-trials-continue-to-decline-in-federal-and-state-courts-does-it-matter/
"Pennsylvania reported the highest civil jury trial disposition rate at 0.53 percent. Other large states reported low civil jury trial rates, including California (0.21 percent), Texas (0.47 percent), Florida (0.18 percent), and New Jersey (0.12 percent). These outcomes were consistent with results from prior years. 
[...]
No state had a civil jury trial rate higher than 0.62 percent (Nebraska), and fully 18 states reported jury trial rates of equal to or less than 0.55 percent, including Pennsylvania (0.55 percent), Texas (0.47 percent), Michigan (0.47 percent), Ohio (0.38 percent), Florida (0.20 percent), and California (0.18 percent).
[...]
In criminal cases, the jury trial rates for four large states (California, Florida, Pennsylvania, and Texas) during the last four years are set forth in Appendix 6 (at right). The jury trial disposition rate was less than 2 percent in all four states and less than 1 percent in California."
[6] https://www.dicindiolaw.com/blog/what-makes-federal-charges-more-severe-than-state-charges
"Most federal criminal offenses carry more severe penalties than the corresponding offenses under state law. Federal indictments often carry harsher penalties because national interests may be at stake.

While some federal offenses do not carry harsher penalties than state charges, most federal criminal convictions will carry longer terms of incarceration, heavier fines, and additional statutory penalties.

For example, federal drug crimes carry lengthy mandatory minimum prison sentences. Federal judges generally follow the Federal Sentencing Guidelines, which establish minimum mandatory sentences and provide different factors that judges can consider to aggravate or mitigate sentences.

People who are convicted of federal crimes are sent to federal prison instead of state prison.

Since the federal government and federal prosecutors generally have more resources and staff available to devote to federal prosecution, defending against federal charges can be more difficult."
[7] https://www.doarlaw.com/blog/2021/04/what-you-should-know-about-the-federal-governments-conviction-rate/
"Data published by the Pew Research Center in 2019 highlighted how federal prosecutors have a 99.6% conviction rate. To put those numbers in perspective, U.S. Attorneys filed 79,704 cases in 2018. Of those, only 320 resulted in acquittals."
[8] https://www.pewresearch.org/fact-tank/2019/06/11/only-2-of-federal-criminal-defendants-go-to-trial-and-most-who-do-are-found-guilty/
"Among the small share and number of federal defendants who went to trial in fiscal 2018, those who opted for a bench trial – that is, one in which the verdict is handed down by a judge – fared better than those who opted for a jury trial. Around four-in-ten defendants who faced a bench trial (38%) were acquitted, compared with just 14% of those who faced a jury trial."
[9] https://ir.lawnet.fordham.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=4637&context=flr
"Rule 23(a) of the Federal Rules of Criminal Procedure (FRCP) provides that "[c]ases required to be tried by jury shall be so tried unless the defendant waives a jury trial in writing with the approval of the court and the consent of the government."' In upholding the validity of this Rule in Singer v. United States," the Supreme Court rejected the claim that the defendant has a right to a trial before a judge.' Additionally, the Court, relying on the integrity of the prosecutor as a "servant of the law," did not require the prosecutor to articulate his reasons for withholding consent to a defendant's requested waiver."
[10] https://harvardlawreview.org/wp-content/uploads/2014/06/vol127_-simonson.pdf
"The jury and the audience fill complementary constitutional roles.
The jury is composed of neutral citizens from the surrounding area without prior knowledge of the case at hand, while the audience contains individuals there to observe or participate in cases in which they have a personal interest. The jury listens to evidence and determines facts and guilt in individual cases, while the audience observes, learns about, and then reacts to what is said and decided inside the courtroom. Taken together, these two constitutionally protected roles allow for local communities to participate in and hold accountable their criminal justice institutions."
[11] https://www.jstor.org/stable/23742035
"Legal scholars today criticize the lack of public participation in the criminal justice system as a barrier to democratic accountability, legitimacy, and fairness. When searching for solutions, these critiques bypass consideration of the audience members who attend criminal court each day — people who fill courtrooms to watch the cases in which their friends, family, and community members have been either victimized or accused of a crime. Overlooking these audience members is a mistake, for the constitutional function of the audience is one uniquely suited to help restore public participation and accountability in a world without juries. The Constitution protects the democratic function of the local audience through both the Sixth Amendment right to a public trial and the First Amendment right of the public to attend criminal court."
[12] https://www.unodc.org/dohadeclaration/en/news/2021/05/the-need-for-independent-judges-and-a-free-press-in-a-democracy.html
"The rule of law is arguably the most basic requirement of any civilized society, and an independent judiciary, to which access is available to all citizens, is an essential ingredient of the rule of law. Freedom of expression is also fundamental in a democratic society, in which the courts and the media have vital and complementary institutional roles. Together, they hold power to account, enforce the rights of individuals and shed light on matters of public interest - and they also monitor each other.

The media have been described as the watchdogs of democracy, highlighting democratic deficits and demanding accountability from elected officials. 
[...]
Governments purporting to defend and promote media freedom, including members of the Media Freedom Coalition in the United Kingdom, should work to maintain the independence and integrity of their own judiciaries, and demonstrate respect for the principle of open justice."
[13] https://digitalcommons.wcl.american.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=1050&context=clb
"The Supreme Court said that in the enumerated constitutional guarantees, there are also “certain unarticulated rights” which are tools for exercising those rights that are spelled out. The right to attend criminal trials, the Court held, is not explicitly stated in the First Amendment but was implicitly guaranteed. The Court held that the freedom of speech clause of First Amendment not only protected the
right to speak, but also the right to ”receive information and ideas.” The other First Amendment guarantee the Court discussed was the right of assembly, which is necessary for meeting the freedom of speech prong of the First Amendment. The right of assembly is protected for a public place, such as a trial court room, “where the people generally—and representatives of the media—have a right to be present, and where their presence historically has been thought to enhance the integrity and quality of what takes place."
[14] https://www.americanbar.org/content/dam/aba-cms-dotorg/products/inv/book/214573/Chapter%201.pdf
"However, most jurors understand that they have a personal stake in ensuring that the system works. As a result, they do an admirable job of applying the law as best they can interpret it to the facts, as best as they are able to understand them.

This is particularly impressive because it is basic human nature to instinctively want to avoid a situation like that which we force them to confront while on jury duty. Most people find it very difficult to make crucial decisions concerning complicated issues about topics of which they initially know very little, decisions that will have a profound impact on the lives of real people. (Just describing it that way gives me a moment of pause.) The fact that jurors have to do this for people they do not know and will likely never see again (as opposed to close family or friends) may make the process a bit easier—but let us not kid ourselves, it is still a very hard process to go through."

"The grand jury acts as an investigative body, acting independently of either prosecuting attorney or judge. Criminal prosecutors present the case to the grand jury. The prosecutors attempt to establish probable cause to believe that a criminal offense has been committed. The grand jury may request that the court compel further evidence, including witness testimony and subpoenas of documents. The grand jury is generally free to pursue its investigations unhindered by external influence or supervision.

 The grand jury assesses whether there is adequate basis for bringing a criminal charge against a suspect. The grand jury is “a kind of buffer or referee between the Government and the people.”"

In contrast, the South Carolina Constitution contains a broader grand jury provision that guarantees the right to an indictment for all state crimes in which the punishment is at least a $200 fine

"The lower federal courts have found a wide variety of prosecutorial actions during a grand jury proceeding to be improper. These include inflammatory or prejudicial remarks by the prosecutor intended either to discredit a witness or to bias the jury; preindictment publicity substantially generated by the prosecutor; use of the grand jury to obtain evidence for another criminal trial or for a civil case; and violation of the grand jury secrecy rules. In addition, federal prosecutors must adhere to the Federal Rules of Criminal Procedure. Most federal courts, however, hesitate to exercise their supervisory power to dismiss an indictment tainted by prosecutorial misconduct."
[28] https://www.moadoph.gov.au/blog/referenda-and-plebiscites-whats-the-difference/#
"Basically, a referendum seeks to amend the Australian Constitution. It is the only way the Constitution can be changed – by a vote of the people. However, it requires a ‘double majority’ – a majority of all voters across the nation and a majority of the states. Voting in referenda is compulsory. Referenda are binding on the government.

A plebiscite is sometimes called an ‘advisory referendum’ because the government does not have to act upon its decision. Plebiscites do not deal with Constitutional questions but issues on which the government seeks approval to act, or not act. As with referenda, plebiscites require an enabling Act of Parliament. Whether or not a plebiscite is to be based on compulsory voting is determined by the enabling Act."
[29] http://cefa.org.au/ccf/are-politicians-keeping-you-uninformed-about-planned-referendums-and-plebiscites
"Only eight out of 44 referendums held in Australia have passed and two of the three plebiscites did not receive a majority result. Many people say this is because the wrong question gets put to the people. The question is ultimately signed off by the Prime Minister and Cabinet. This means that the wording of the questions can be subjective and can very much reflect the personal intention of the writers and approvers and without a doubt affect the referendum or plebiscite result.

So, independently of the Yes and No campaigns, the question asked of the people at the time of a plebiscite or referendum has a huge impact on the result. This is why we all need to be informed and engaged with the debate up until the time that the question is crafted, not just at the end when the question is asked. It is vitally important that everyone has an understanding of our Constitution so that the right model and questions are proposed to the people."

[...]
In Australia, a plebiscite (also known as an advisory referendum) is used to decide a national question that does not affect the Constitution. It can be used to test whether the government has enough public support to go ahead with a proposed action. Unlike a referendum, the decision reached in a plebiscite does not have any legal force."

[35] https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/burden_of_proof
"Generally, describes the standard that a party seeking to prove a fact in court must satisfy to have that fact legally established. There are different standards in different circumstances. For example, in criminal cases, the burden of proving the defendant’s guilt is on the prosecution, and they must establish that fact beyond a reasonable doubt. In civil cases, the plaintiff has the burden of proving his case by a preponderance of the evidence. A "preponderance of the evidence" and "beyond a reasonable doubt" are different standards, requiring different amounts of proof.

The burden of proof is often said to consist of two distinct but related concepts: the burden of production, and the burden of persuasion."
[36] https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/making-sense-chaos/202111/abolish-the-jury
"On paper, the jury is the paragon of democracy. But is it really?

The concept of the jury trial is often credited to King Henry II, who convened groups of 12 “free and lawful” men to decide disputes over land. The expansion of the British Empire spread the jury system throughout the globe with the reach of the setting sun. But, most Americans don’t know that their jury system is now an anomaly in the developed world.

The jury system was abolished in Germany in 1924, Singapore and South Africa in 1969, and India in 1973. Today, even in those countries where the jury system still exists, it is used only sparingly or as part of a “mixed court” that includes both laypersons and judges. The vast majority of all jury trials that occur today are held within the United States."
[37] https://uk.practicallaw.thomsonreuters.com/w-007-7132?transitionType=Default&contextData=\(sc.Default\)&firstPage=true
"Civil law
There are no juries in civil proceedings. Proceedings before specialised commercial courts are conducted by a panel consisting of one career judge and two commercial lay judges, who are specially appointed business people (section 109, Court Constitution Act (Gerichtsverfassungsgesetz)). The use of lay judges is intended to ensure the appropriate consideration of commercial usages, the standards of a fair and diligent businessperson and so on.
[...]
Criminal law
Juries are not used in criminal proceedings either. Minor cases are tried by one career judge, and other matters are tried by a court consisting of one career judge and two lay judges (section 29, Court Constitution Act). In certain very serious matters (for example, cases involving the death of the victim), the court consists of three career judges and two lay judges (section 74, Court Constitution Act), Lay judges in criminal proceedings are meant to ensure the approval of a criminal verdict by the public."

[39] http://blog.practicalethics.ox.ac.uk/2021/05/a-jurors-guide-to-going-rogue/
"A jury recently acquitted several activists charged with causing £25,000 worth of damage to Shell’s HQ in London despite the defendants admitting that they caused the damage and the judge informing the jury that the defendants had no legal defence. In other words, if the law were applied correctly, the jury had no choice but to find them guilty. 
[...]
Are juries allowed to behave in this way? The short answer is ‘yes’ but they are strongly discouraged from even considering it. Juries are typically instructed by the judge to determine the veracity and significance of the evidence, apply that evidence to the law, and reach a verdict without allowing sympathy to compromise their evaluation. They aren’t supposed to question the law itself. Furthermore, if the lawyers arguing the case suggest to the jury that they are not required to apply the law when coming to a verdict, they are in contempt of court and a mistrial may be declared."
[40] https://www.nolo.com/legal-encyclopedia/what-jury-nullification.html
"Jury nullification takes place when jurors acquit a defendant who is factually guilty because they disagree with the law as written. 

For example, during Prohibition, juries who disagreed with alcohol control laws often acquitted defendants who had been caught red handed smuggling alcohol."
[41] https://fija.org/library-and-resources/library/called-for-jury-duty.html
"As a juror, you have the right to vote your conscience, even if it means setting aside the law to conscientiously acquit someone who has technically broken the law. 

You cannot legally be punished for or required to change your verdict. In fact, there is no requirement for jurors to deliver a verdict. If jurors cannot agree on a verdict, judges may issue additional instructions known as "Allen" or "dynamite" charges. These additional instructions may strongly imply to jurors that they are somehow remiss in their duty if they do not reach a verdict. This is simply not true.

Jurors should not give up their conscientiously held beliefs under such pressure just for the sake of consensus. If they cannot reach a verdict, the judge will at some point declare a mistrial."
[42] https://itshamiltime.com/2013/01/28/hamilton-on-jury-nullification/
"Judges will not tell you about your right to nullify—to vote not guilty regardless of whether the prosecution has proven its case if you believe the law at issue is unjust. They may tell you that you may only judge the facts of the case put to you and not the law. They may strike you from a jury if you do not agree under oath to do so, but the right to nullify exists. There is reason to be concerned about this power: nobody wants courtroom anarchy. But there is also reason to wield it, especially today: if you believe that nonviolent drug offenders should not go to prison, vote not guilty."
[43] https://www.lewrockwell.com/2010/02/derek-sheriff/the-untold-history-of-nullification-resistingslavery/
"In 1851, 26 people in Syracuse, New York were arrested, charged and tried for freeing a runaway slave named William Henry (aka Jerry) who had been arrested under the Fugitive Slave Act. Among the 26 people tried was a U.S. Senator and the former Governor of New York! In an act of jury nullification, the trial resulted in only one conviction. “Jerry” was hidden in Syracuse for several days until he could safely escape into Canada."

"On August 28, 1955, while visiting family in Money, Mississippi, 14-year-old Emmett Till, an African American from Chicago, is brutally murdered for allegedly flirting with a white woman four days earlier.
[...]
Less than two weeks after Emmett’s body was buried, Milam and Bryant went on trial in a segregated courthouse in Sumner, Mississippi. There were few witnesses besides Mose Wright, who positively identified the defendants as Emmett’s killers.

On September 23, the all-white jury deliberated for less than an hour before issuing a verdict of “not guilty,” explaining that they believed the state had failed to prove the identity of the body. Many people around the country were outraged by the decision and also by the state’s decision not to indict Milam and Bryant on the separate charge of kidnapping."

"Nullification was evident during the Vietnam War (when selective service protesters were acquitted by juries opposed to the war) and currently appears in criminal cases when the jury disagrees with the punishment -- for example, in "three strikes" cases when the jury realizes that conviction of a relatively minor offense will result in lifetime imprisonment."
[47] https://www.washingtonpost.com/archive/opinions/1999/02/20/renegade-jurors/1d1235de-5fb5-473c-ab04-724e2e3b7f27/
"Likewise, jury nullification was used extensively during the days of the fugitive slave act in refusing to convict those aiding escaped slaves. Allowing the jury power to acquit despite the law has been the historical default for at least 700 years prior to this century. The government changed procedures in the 19th century after juries refused to convict union members on conspiracy charges for organizing labor unions. But even up to the past few decades, federal courts were affirming the historical right of juries to nullify unjust laws."
[48] https://www.findlaw.com/criminal/criminal-procedure/jury-nullification.html
"Modern juries perform two important functions in criminal trials. They are first the finders of fact, meaning that they can evaluate the evidence and credibility of witnesses. Potential jurors are then called upon to apply the law to those facts. The applicable law is given to them by the trial judge in the form of jury instructions. Jurors swear an oath to follow those instructions and fulfill their duty impartially. Jury nullification is what happens when juries disregard that oath because they do not agree with the law.
[...]
In other contexts, a jury might refuse to convict out of concern for the consequences of conviction or an unwillingness to see a minimum sentence imposed. In these examples, the jurors may be violating their oaths and abdicating their responsibilities"
[49] https://web.archive.org/web/20171019190646/http://fija.org/docs/BR_YYYY_surviving_voir_dire.pdf
"When a jury acquits because the law is unconscionable, it is called jury nullification of the law - or, in short, jury nullification. Jury nullification occurs in 3-4% of all criminal trials"
[50] https://www.nolo.com/legal-encyclopedia/what-jury-nullification.html

"The 21st Amendment to the U.S. Constitution is ratified, repealing the 18th Amendment and bringing an end to the era of national prohibition of alcohol in America. [...]

On January 16, 1919, the 18th Amendment was ratified by the states. Prohibition went into effect the next year, on January 17, 1920."

"When a jury acquits a defendant even though he or she clearly appears to be guilty, the acquittal conveys significant information about community attitudes and provides a guideline for future prosecutorial discretion in the enforcement of the laws. Because of the high acquittal rate in prohibition cases during the 1920s and early 1930s, prohibition laws could not be enforced. The repeal of these laws is traceable to the refusal of juries to convict those accused of alcohol traffic."

"A trial jury is supposed to serve only as a "fact finder": that is, to evaluate the veracity of witnesses and the strength of evidence presented at trial, then apply the law to that evidence in order to reach a verdict. The jury isn't supposed to decide what the law is—or what it should be."

"Thus, voir dire can be a vital instrument to probe whether or not a potential juror may tend to refuse to follow the law and thus can help prevent jury nullification.

The court must uproot potential jurors' tendencies to ignore the law prior to trial. At the conclusion of trial, the court cannot require the jury to give a justification of its verdict. "Juries thus can introduce into the law a flexibility and responsiveness to special circumstances that may [seem to] be important to its public acceptance." [62] https://www.bigwas.com/2019/02/justifying-vs-exempting-circumstances.html
"Justifying circumstance affects the act, not the actor; while exempting circumstance affects the actor, not the act. In justifying circumstance, no criminal and, generally no civil liability incurred; while in exempting circumstance, civil liability is generally incurred although there is no criminal liability."
[63] https://openyls.law.yale.edu/bitstream/handle/20.500.13051/8048/05_22YaleJonReg1_2005_.pdf?sequence=2&isAllowed=y
"While the bar has generally resisted outside regulation, its opposition to many of the lawyer regulation initiatives under Sarbanes-Oxley reflects a misguided attachment to the privileges of non-accountability associated with deliberate ignorance and calculated ambiguity. To restore integrity to the profession, it must break this attachment."
[64] https://attyrcd.wordpress.com/2010/01/28/justifying-and-exempting-circumstances-2/
"Justifying circumstances are those wherein the acts of the actor are in accordance with law and, hence, he incurs no criminal and civil liability. 
[...]
Exempting circumstances are those wherein there is an absence in the agent of the crime of all the condition that would make an act voluntary and, hence, although there is no criminal liability, there is civil liability. In exempting, the crime is committed but there is absent in the person of the offender any element of voluntariness, and so he is not criminally liable but is civilly liable except in the exempting circumstances of accident and lawful or insuperable cause."
[65] https://www.projectjurisprudence.com/2018/06/uncontrolled-fear-exempting-circumstance.html
"For uncontrollable fear to be considered as an exempting circumstance, it must appear that the threat that caused the uncontrollable fear is of such gravity and imminence that the ordinary man would have succumbed to it. It should be based on a real, imminent or reasonable fear for one’s life or limb. A mere threat of a future injury is not enough. It should not be speculative, fanciful, or remote.

A person invoking uncontrollable fear must show therefore that the compulsion was such that it reduced him to a mere instrument acting not only without will but against his will as well. It must be of such character as to leave no opportunity to the accused for escape."
[66] https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/justifiable_homicide
"The taking of a human life under circumstances of justification, as a matter of right, such as self-defense, or other causes set out in statute. For example, in Virginia, a justifiable homicide in self-defense occurs where a person, without any fault on his part in provoking or bringing on the difficulty, kills another under reasonable apprehension of death or great bodily harm to himself. Justifiable homicides also include killings permitted by law, such as an execution for a capital crime. A justifiable homicide absolves the actor of any criminal liability. Justifiable homicides are not the same as homicides committed under the heat of passion or with diminished capacity, which may be considered mitigating circumstances that reduce the actor’s culpability with regards to a killing."
[67] https://www.encyclopedia.com/law/legal-and-political-magazines/justification-theory
"The defense of insanity, for example, does not seek to justify the violation of a norm. Rather, the defense concedes that the violation is unjustified, but seeks to exempt the particular actor from responsibility for the unjustified act. A claim of justification maintains that the act is right; a claim of excuse concedes that the act in the abstract is wrong, but argues that the actor is not personally responsible for having committed the act.

Thus, the issue of justification appears as the second in a set of three ordered questions bearing on criminal liability: (1) Did the suspect's act violate a valid norm of the criminal law? (2) Is the violation of the norm unlawful (justified)? (3) Is the actor personally accountable for the unlawful violation; that is, is the unlawful violation unexcused? A negative answer to any of these three questions terminates the inquiry into liability."
[68] https://www.libertarianism.org/columns/are-judges-bound-enforce-unjust-laws

"Judicial independence—the ability to decide court cases fairly and impartially without fear of punishment and without control or influence by the executive or legislative branches, is not a Democrat versus Republican issue. Nor is it a liberal versus conservative issue. Threats to judicial independence have arisen from the right and the left, and judicial independence has been defended by those on the right and the left. Judicial independence is part of the DNA of American judicial systems—state and federal. It is one of the features that make our courts models for other democracies. In our federal system, judicial independence is grounded in the structure of the national government established under the U.S. Constitution that provides for the separation of powers and allocates the “judicial power of the United States” to the judicial branch."
[74] https://legaljobs.io/blog/what-is-common-law/
"Common law isn’t a set of formal statutes. Instead, it’s based on court-established legal precedents. Verdicts by public juries and judicial authorities are institutionalized and serve as a foundation for any relevant future instances. It’s also referred to as case law, as it’s the law created by judges for decisions on individual cases or disputes.
[...]
Common law refers to a detailed record of previous court cases, especially when no formal statute can be applied to a particular circumstance. It’s up to the presiding judge to resolve which precedents are relevant for a given case.

Any legal precedent set by the higher court is legally binding for any cases that go through lower courts. In this manner, universal legal rules are established across the US justice system.
[...]
A lower common law court can also, on rare occasions, overturn an established precedent."

"Lawyers know judges and know how to reach them outside the courtroom. Judges are also usually low-paid and face a huge caseload, so bribery can make their life easier by reducing the need for deliberation. Juries are different: Lawyers will not know jury members personally, and do not have the kind of relationships with each juror as they have with judges. Attempting to track down all of the jurors on a panel in order to bribe them would be time-consuming and expensive. And jurors are compensated for being part of a jury, on top of their normal salary, giving them less incentive to take bribes."
[102] https://rm.coe.int/corruption-prevention-members-of-parliament-judges-and-prosecutors-con/16807638e7

This of course highlights the importance of safeguarding the independence of judges, and the risks associated with dysfunction within the court system and within the body of judges."

"Both state and federal judges face sanctions for corruption from primarily three institutions:

(1) impeachment by Congress or the state legislature;
(2) criminal prosecution by federal or state authorities; and
(3) censure by the federal judicial councils (FJCs) or, in the case of state judges, censure or removal by state JCOs.

While impeachment by the legislative branch was originally the preferred method of accountability for both state and federal judges, this time­ consuming and political-capital-draining process has been used less frequently as the obligations of Congress and state legislators have grown.

The criminal prosecution of judges, which only became an accepted practice with regard to Article III judges after 1973, has been filling this void."

[115] https://www.findlaw.com/criminal/criminal-charges/perjury.html
"[J]udges often base their verdicts, sentences, or other important decisions on sworn testimony and signed documents. Statements given under oath and certain legal documents are presumed to be truthful, or at least made in good faith. But how do we know for sure that witnesses and other parties involved in a legal matter are telling the truth? We can’t always be certain, but those who are caught knowingly misleading a court face serious criminal charges of perjury.

To “perjure” yourself is to knowingly make misleading or false statements under oath or to sign a legal document you know to be false or misleading. This crime is taken very seriously because the foundation of the legal system depends on trust and credibility. After all, just one sworn statement has the power to tip the scales of justice and dramatically alter someone’s life.

Perjury is considered a crime against justice, since lying under oath compromises the authority of courts, grand juries, [and] governing bodies."

Jury misconduct has been found as the result of juror disobedience to instructions, inappropriate communications with court personnel, third parties and/or alternate jurors, contact with extraneous reading materials and/or inadmissible evidence, race and ethnic prejudice, improper jury deliberations and official misconduct by bailiffs, judges and other court personnel."
[118] https://digitalcommons.lmu.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=1008&context=elr
"The advancement of technology has recently allowed juror misconduct to evolve from such traditional instances of investigating the crime scene or talking about the case with one's spouse to more sophisticated means of Internet-based misconduct. At any given moment, jurors now have the ability to use their cellular telephones to browse the web for the names of attorneys or parties in a case, educate themselves through Wikipedia about the technology underlying a patent claim or medical condition, examine an intersection using Google Maps, or even blog and update their friends about the case through Facebook and Twitter. The New York Times has coined the phrase "Google Mistrial" to refer to mistrials that result when jurors use their iPhones and Blackberrys to receive and transmit information about the cases they sit on. This type of behavior has recently been "wreaking havoc on trials around the country, upending deliberations and infuriating judges.""
